| lost | The graphics card stopped responding (it was reset, or the browser took it back). Try again. |
| out-of-memory | The graphics card ran out of memory. Try a smaller size, or close other tabs and apps that use graphics. |
| validation | The graphics card rejected the work for this photo. Try a smaller size; if it keeps happening, please report it. |
| internal | The graphics card reported an internal error. Try again. |

No help text described the old messages, so none changed.

### Export

| Situation | Before (main) | Now |
|---|---|---|
| A command buffer fails (an invalid intermediate stands in for out of memory) | File written, every pixel 0, counted as saved | That photo fails with the validation message, no file; the others are written |
| 16-bit export over the device's buffer limits | Files written, all black | Fails up front with the size message, no file |
| Device lost once during a photo | That photo fails with "Failed to execute 'mapAsync'…", every later one with "Reload the page" | The photo is retried on a fresh engine and written; later photos are fine |
| Device lost on every attempt | — | Each photo fails with the lost message, no files |
| Engine found lost before a photo | Every later photo failed | A new engine is made before the photo |

Each photo's load, process and render run in one `guarded` call (`export/run.ts`); the photo is unloaded after its render, before encoding.

### One present probe per page

| Behaviour | Detail |
|---|---|
| When | `engine/gpu/present.ts`; `main.tsx` starts it at start-up, before any GPU work. It runs at most once per page and its answer is kept. |
| What | One frame on a 1×1 WebGPU canvas of a device of its own (destroyed afterwards); if that device is lost, read-back. |
| Skipped | `?gpu=readback` / `?gpu=canvas` decide without a test (as before). A fallback (software) adapter (`adapter.info.isFallbackAdapter`, or the older `adapter.isFallbackAdapter`) uses read-back without a test. No adapter: read-back. |
| Users | `DevelopEngine.create('auto')`, `DevelopRenderer.create('auto')` (System check), and the System check's message, which now says why it is in compatibility mode (chosen in the address, software graphics, no adapter, or the test failed). |
| No device while it runs | Every `DevelopEngine.create` (all modes) waits for the probe first. |

### Recovery

| Component | After a lost device (before → now) |
|---|---|
| Develop | Showed the error; never recreated within a mount → `onLost` bumps an `engineGen` state; a new engine is made, the photo shown is uploaded again from the decode Develop keeps and developed with its current settings (unsaved changes included) before anything uses the engine; a photo still loading is loaded again. The lost canvas is removed. A notice for 8 s: "The graphics card was reset, so Develop restarted it. Your edit is safe." Errors caused by the loss aren't shown. A read-back canvas is redrawn on `contextrestored`. The Tone Curve's table is per engine and is built again by the new engine's first drawing. |
| Background developer | New engine for the next photo; the photo in flight was a problem → Its GPU steps are guarded and the failed step is retried once on a fresh engine: working out the settings (nothing saved yet), or, after the edit was saved, only the thumbnail. So a retry never adds a second history step, and a photo that works on the retry is not reported. If the thumbnail fails twice: "Settings applied; the thumbnail wasn't refreshed. …" |
| Library 100 % view (`FullRenderer`) | Never reset → replaces a lost engine before a render (as the background developer does), guards the full-size read-back, retries once on a fresh engine. |
| Export | One engine per run → as above. |

### Less GPU memory

| Behaviour | Detail |
|---|---|
| Foreground gate | `engine/gpu/gate.ts`. Develop holds it while it loads and develops a photo, re-runs the RAW stage, draws, and measures the histogram and the Tone Curve's input. The background developer (before each GPU step), the 100 % renderer and Export (before each photo) wait until Develop has been idle for 150 ms. |
| Can't starve | A background step waits at most 2 s (Export: 1 s), then goes ahead even if Develop is still busy, so a long slider drag doesn't stop a Paste or an Export. |
| Can't deadlock | Only background work waits, and only between steps, never while holding anything Develop waits for; Develop never waits for background work. A hold that is never released (a drawing that hangs) ends after 30 s. |
| `unload()` | Frees the scene, its warped and lens-corrected copies, the retouched copy, the tone masks, the Detail stage's results and their warps (Part 13), the Tone Curve's table, and the uploaded RAW buffer or JPEG pixels; the engine keeps its pipelines. Called after each background-developer photo, after each 100 % render and after each exported photo's render. Never on Develop's engine. |

### With Detail (Part 13)

| Behaviour | Detail |
|---|---|
| Guarded | Export, the background developer and the 100 % view run the Detail stage inside their `guarded` call (it is part of developing and rendering the photo), so a failed Detail pass fails or retries the photo like any other GPU work. |
| After a lost device | The Detail stage's kernel table and pipelines belong to the engine; a new engine makes them again on its first Detail run, with its per-run buffers (binding 15, the separable blur's horizontal pass, included). Develop's recovery uploads and develops the photo again, so its next drawing runs Detail again. |
| `unload()` | Drops the Detail results and their warps with the photo; the kernel table (small) and pipelines stay. |
| Gate | Detail work in the background (the batch's thumbnails, Auto, the 100 % view, Export) runs in the steps that already wait on the gate. The noise-profile download only fetches data; the drawing it starts in Develop goes through Develop's render loop, which holds the gate. |
| Buffer limits | The Detail stage checks its own full-size buffers against the device's limits before it allocates them (Part 13: "The photo is too large for this GPU's buffers for noise reduction …"); that plain error passes through `guarded` and is shown as it is. Export's output-buffer check is separate and unchanged. |
| Waiting for Develop's engine | Found in the S2 run after merging Detail: opening a photo gave up with "Graphics acceleration is not available." when the engine took more than 5 s to make. The photo's load now waits as long as an engine is being made (also a new one after a loss), up to 2 minutes. |

### Replaced textures freed while drawings overlap; a cheaper picker loupe (0.6.6 freeze report)

Report (2026-10-19): on Windows, a 24 MP Nikon D5200 RAW at fit, a few clicks with the white balance picker progressively froze the whole computer (no driver reset; a hard power-off). A second freeze came while changing noise reduction at 100 %. Task Manager showed Chrome running LightBench on the laptop's **integrated Intel UHD Graphics** (GPU 1, at 100 %), not its NVIDIA card, although the engine asks for `powerPreference: 'high-performance'`. An integrated GPU's memory is the computer's main memory (RAM), and it also draws the desktop, so work and memory piling up there can stall the whole computer without a driver reset.

| Change | Detail |
|---|---|
| Retirement per lease (`engine/gpu/retire.ts`) | Before, a cached texture replaced while any drawing was open (a scene rebuilt for a new white balance, a Detail result, a warp) waited until **no** drawing at all was open, and the whole list was destroyed only then. With the canvas, the histogram and curve measures, the loupe and the previews overlapping, that moment can be postponed for as long as the user keeps editing, and every replaced full-size texture (at 24 MP about 250 MB each with its mips) stays allocated meanwhile. Now a replaced texture waits only for the drawings open when it was replaced (a later drawing can't pick it: it is no longer cached) and is destroyed when the last of those ends, so continuous overlapping drawing can't defer it. The same rule replaces the Remove stage's own list (`retouch-stage.ts`). The 20 s lease timeout stays. WebGPU keeps a destroyed texture's memory until work already submitted with it has finished, so destroying at the end of a lease is safe. |
| The picker's loupe (`renderLoupe`) | Each pointer move draws a 15 × 15 px crop at 100 %. It asked for the **full-size Detail result**, so the first move after each pick (a new white balance rebuilds a RAW's scene and drops its Detail results) started a full-size Detail run (Part 16: about 100 ms and 1.5 GB at its peak at 24 MP on a real GPU), and it evicted the fitted view's Detail result, which the next drawing then made again. The loupe now uses the full-size Detail result only when it is already there (the view at 100 %, or an edit with spots, which needs it anyway) and otherwise shows the scene without Detail. The picked value never used Detail (it samples the scene), so picks are unchanged; the loupe's pixels at fit differ slightly (no noise reduction or sharpening in the loupe). |
| Memory accounting | `DevelopEngine.memoryStats()`: bytes of the textures and buffers the engine's device holds (estimated from sizes, formats and mips by wrapping its `createTexture`/`createBuffer`), the replaced textures still waiting (count, bytes) and the drawings open. Shown by the Diagnostics readout (below). Integrated or discrete makes no difference to the rule: nothing waits longer than the drawings that may use it. |

Measured on SwiftShader (`?gpu=readback`, headless Chromium; not the owner's hardware), driving Develop's engine as Develop does (one coalesced render loop, one stats loop, one loupe loop), live GPU bytes counted for every texture and buffer the page made:

| Run | main 0.6.6 | this branch |
|---|---|---|
| D80 RAW (10 MP), 3 picker cycles at fit (8 pointer moves, a click, the RAW stage rebuilt with the picked white balance) | first loupe **136 s**, peak **1478 MB**, 421 MB live afterwards; the 3 cycles took 799 s | first loupe **16 s**, peak **696 MB**, 188 MB live afterwards; 349 s |
| The same photo, Detail sliders (3 rounds at fit, then 100 %) | at fit: peak 583 MB, 421 MB live when idle; at 100 %: 602 MB live, peak 1251 MB; replaced textures waiting at most 1 at any sample | at fit: peak 350 MB, 188 MB live when idle; at 100 %: 369 MB live, peak 1018 MB (the 100 % rounds were cut short by a container restart); waiting at most 0 |
| 0.4 MP JPEG, a change every 50 ms for 60 s at fit and 60 s at 100 %, with the loupe loop running too | waiting textures at most **2**, back to 0 after each gap; 11 MB live at the end | at most **1**; 11 MB live at the end; the same final pixels (512 px render SHA-256 `97c8a031…` on both) |

Main's memory went back down after every phase (what stays is the scene and the results the engine caches by design: Detail at two levels, warps; on the branch less of it at fit because the loupe no longer leaves a full-size Detail result behind), so on SwiftShader the "never freed" build-up was **not** reproduced: its drawings take seconds, the loops coalesce, and some moment with no drawing open came within a few seconds (on SwiftShader a lease also often ran into its 20 s timeout during a full-size Detail run). Whether a real GPU, with many short overlapping drawings, kept a drawing open long enough to pile textures up is not known; `retire.test.ts` shows the new rule keeping at most one texture waiting through 50 continuously overlapping drawings that each replace one; under the old rule none of the 50 would have been freed before the last drawing ended. What was measured is the picker's loupe running full-size Detail on every pick, which the change removes. A full-size Detail run at 100 % (the second report) still needs its 1.5 GB peak at 24 MP once per change; nothing here makes that smaller.

Checks on this change: **old edits unchanged** (`identity.mjs`, Part 17's 15 edits on two NEFs, an ARW and the JPEG, with Diagnostics off): **90 of 90 identical** to main (full-size and 1024 px export SHA-256, 512 px thumbnail, histogram, Develop canvas at fit and at 100 %; main's hashes from the same script on main `44c29cd`, whose rendering code is that of this branch's base `22be61b`; the last edit hit the known transient OPFS `NotFoundError` in the full run and was run again alone). **Picker in the UI** (`e2e-picker.mjs`, the JPEG and the D80 NEF, two picks each at the same points): the same Temp/Tint on main and the branch for all four picks; the loupe's RGB readout matched in three and differed in one at fit (65.5/73.3/83.9 % vs 70.2/76.5/86.3 %: the loupe without Detail, or a different pointer step drawn). Unit tests: `retire.test.ts` (immediate destroy, waiting only for the leases open at retirement, 50 overlapping drawings with at most one texture waiting, the timeout, `destroyAll`, texture sizes and the device counter).

### The Remove brush's pins (found while verifying this part)

| Behaviour | Detail |
|---|---|
| Before | The overlay's pins combined the photo's box from the last drawing made while the brush was on (React state) with the engine's map from sensor to screen, which changes when the next drawing *starts*. Opened after Rotate right or a crop, the brush used a box from before the change with the map of the drawing being made, so the pins sat where no drawing put them until that drawing ended (about 25 s on loaded SwiftShader). That is what made `e2e-remove.mjs` fail "turned right" and "cropped 1:1": stale pins, not misplaced fills. |
| Now | After each drawing Develop keeps its box and a snapshot of its map together (`DevelopEngine.shownSnapshot()`; `engine/develop/shown-map.ts`, pure `displayToSensorAt` / `sensorToDisplayAt`), and the overlay maps through that pair. Opening the brush shows the last finished drawing's pair at once, and every drawing that ends while the brush is on (also one that started before it was opened) replaces it. So the pins always match the photo on screen, also while the next drawing is being made. The engine's own `displayToSensor` / `sensorToDisplay` work as before, through the same functions. |

### Verified

**After merging main `a0f8353`** (0.5.4, Detail), against that main. Identity, export failures and the first S2 run were on the merge `d049830`; the final code `b80b9b0` only changes how Develop waits for its engine, and S2, the Detail recovery and Remove were run on it:
- **Identity, engine level** (`identity.mjs`, now 19 cases: the 15 below plus Detail sharpening and noise reduction with a Tone Curve, sharpening with spots, Radius/Masking with Color noise reduction and an angle, and sharpening on the JPEG; the two RAW "defaults" cases carry the new RAW sharpening): **19 of 19 identical** (full-size and 1024 px export SHA-256, 512 px thumbnail, histogram). In the full run the last two cases failed on both builds alike with an OPFS `NotFoundError` reading the file; run again on their own they were identical on both.
- **Export failures** (`probe-export.mjs`): the same results as below on this branch (validation: no file, the message for people; capped limits: the size message; one loss: retried and written; every time: the lost message, no files); main `a0f8353` still writes the black files and gives the raw messages.
- **S2 without `?gpu=readback`**: "3 photos updated", no problems. In the first run Develop then showed "Graphics acceleration is not available." (its engine took more than 5 s to make while the batch ran); a retry passed (the device took 1.2 s), and with the fix above the final code passed (Develop drawn; Develop's device took 2.0 s). Main `a0f8353` with `?gpu=readback` also passed.
- **Develop after `device.destroy()`**: 14/14 (`e2e-recover.mjs`, two rounds). **With Detail** (`e2e-recover-detail.mjs`, the D700 NEF at 100 %, sharpness as the mean |Laplacian| of the canvas's luma): Amount 150 raised it from 5.473 to 7.275; after the loss the new engine drew it at exactly 7.275 again, and Amount 0 gave 5.473 again (5/5). The first run measured at fit, where sharpening is finer than the screen, and failed that first check (9.204 vs 9.323 off); the redraw after the loss was identical there too.
- **Remove** (`e2e-remove.mjs`, corrected script): **18 of 18** on this branch and on main `a0f8353`, with sharpening set to 0 on the photo and the effect measured only once the stage is no longer `aria-busy`. With the new RAW sharpening left on, both builds fail the same four "fill under the pin" checks with the same numbers (e.g. effect centroid 707.1,560.3 over 2322 px instead of 439 px): with spots the Detail stage runs at full size, without them at the view's level (Part 13), so hiding the spots changes the whole view slightly and the measurement, not the fill, moves. Without the `aria-busy` wait, the branch failed "turned right" twice with the centroid at the canvas centre (two different geometries compared); main passed it in its one complete run. The pins read at once differ as before: main put them at 524.9,396.6 after Rotate right and at 608.5,335.7 after the crop (a mix of two drawings); the branch at 556.6,594.8 and 608.5,385.9, the drawings on screen.
- `npm ci`, `npx tsc -b`, `npx oxlint` (the two existing warnings), `npx vitest run` (75 files, 730 tests; main `a0f8353` has 69 and 688, so 42 new) and `npm run build` passed.

**Before that merge** (main `993f81b`):

Headless Chromium 141 with SwiftShader WebGPU (which reports a fallback adapter), Vite dev builds of main `993f81b` (0.5.3, with the Tone Curve) and this branch merged with it, served side by side. Scripts in the scratchpad's `gpurob/` folder (copies of the diagnoses' and earlier parts' scripts). Earlier runs against main `60b1170` before the merge gave the same results where they overlap.

- **Identity of old edits, engine level** (`identity.mjs`, 15 cases on the D700 and D80 NEFs, a 24 MP Sony ARW and `test-srgb.jpg`, including crop/turn/angle, custom white balance, three edits with Remove/Heal/Clone spots and three with a Tone Curve, one with spots too): full-size 8-bit export SHA-256, 1024 px export SHA-256, 512 px thumbnail and histogram statistics all **identical to main, 15 of 15**. Also identical, 15 of 15, with **one engine reused for every case and `unload()` between cases**.
- **Identity through the app's code paths** (`export-identity.mjs`, saved edits with spots and a Tone Curve, fixed clock because the ICC profile holds the export time): `runExport` as full-size JPEG, 16-bit PNG at 1024 px and 8-bit TIFF at 50 %: **9 of 9 files byte-identical** to main; a Paste through the background developer: the three saved thumbnails and settings identical.
- **Export failures** (`probe-export.mjs`, the diagnosis's probe through the real `runExport`, two copies of the JPEG; mean pixel value of each written file):

  | Case | Main 993f81b | This branch |
  |---|---|---|
  | Baseline | 2 written, mean 142.37 | Same |
  | Invalid intermediate on one photo | Written, mean 0 (black) | Fails with the validation message, no file; the other written, 142.37 |
  | Device capped at default limits, 16-bit PNG at 400 % | 2 written, 6400×4288, mean 0 | Both fail with the size message, no files |
  | Device destroyed once after the submit | Both fail ("mapAsync…", "Reload the page") | Retried; both written, 142.37 |
  | Device destroyed on every attempt | Both fail, same raw messages | Both fail with the lost message, no files |
- **The diagnosis's S2 scenario without `?gpu=readback`** (`s2.mjs`: a Paste on three photos running in the Library, then Develop opened on a 24 MP ARW): "3 photos updated.", no problems, Develop drawn. SwiftShader reports a fallback adapter, so the probe was skipped (read-back, "fallback-adapter"). With the adapter made to report a hardware one (an init script), the probe ran at start-up: its own device was lost 28 ms after it was made ("probe-failed", read-back), no other device was lost, and the same run ended "3 photos updated." with no problems. On main `993f81b` the same plain run ended "2 photos updated, 1 with problems … C.ARW: A valid external Instance reference no longer exists." (as in the diagnosis).
- **Develop after `device.destroy()`** (`e2e-recover.mjs`, the D700 NEF, 14/14): twice in a row, the notice showed, the photo was drawn again on a new canvas with the same mean, no error message, one canvas and one live device; Before/After and an exposure change still drew. With a Tone Curve (Strong Contrast) on the photo (`e2e-recover-curve.mjs`, 9/9): drawn again with the curve (mean 91.8, as before the loss; 94.0 without the curve).
- **Remove** (`e2e-remove.mjs`, the D700 and D80 NEFs): with the earlier script, 16 of 18 on this branch and on main `993f81b`, failing "turned right" and "cropped 1:1" at the same coordinates on both: stale pins (above). With the corrected script (it reads the pins with the brush opened on the finished drawing, and logs the pins read at once while the redraw after the change is still pending): **18 of 18** after the pin fix, including the background developer's Paste. The pins read at once differ: with the fix, after Rotate right they were at 556.6,594.8, where the fill was on the unrotated photo still on screen (556.0,595.1); main's were at 524.9,396.6, where no drawing put them. The corrected script also passes 18 of 18 on main, because it waits for the redraw.
- **Mapping unit tests** (`engine/develop/shown-map.test.ts`, 4): sensor → screen → sensor and back round trips through Rotate right, a crop and an angle, with and without a lens correction (the Sony 7M2's camera data); the lens correction is used; a snapshot is mapped through, not the latest drawing.
- **Tone Curve** (`e2e-curve.mjs`, `e2e-curve2.mjs`, `test-srgb.jpg`): run alone, **35/35** on this branch without `?gpu=readback`, and 35/35 on main with it; main without it gave 29/35 (its Develop canvas read almost black in the failing checks, R 2.3 of 255; the cause wasn't looked into further). `e2e-curve2.mjs` (copy, paste, presets, Lightroom sidecars): 12/12 on this branch. Run with three other browsers at once, `e2e-curve.mjs` failed 2 timing-sensitive checks on this branch, different ones in each of three runs (clipping after a drag, a point drag, a point add, the Red curve, the eye), and main failed 10 in the same conditions; those runs are not counted.
- **Memory** (`mem.mjs`, the diagnosis's method: the GPU process's RSS sampled every 500 ms; 24 MP ARWs, both builds with `?gpu=readback` so main loses no devices): Develop open on one ARW while a Paste runs on three others and Develop redraws every 4 s; then, in the Library, one 100 % render.

  | GPU-process RSS (MB) | Main 993f81b | This branch |
  |---|---|---|
  | Idle, photos added | 124 | 128 |
  | Develop showing the ARW | 1390 | 1394 |
  | **Peak while the Paste ran with Develop open** | **2550** | **2062** |
  | Lowest during the Paste | 777 | 772 |
  | After the Paste, Develop open | 762 | 764 |
  | Library, Develop closed | 204 | 205 |
  | Peak during the 100 % render | 1446 | 1444 |
  | After the 100 % render, renderer open | 1198 | 1195 |
  | Renderer disposed | 276 | 274 |
  | Paste duration | 710 s | 397 s |

  One run each, with other browsers running at the same time (timings are not comparable). The peak with Develop open fell by 488 MB; this run can't tell how much of that is the gate (each Paste step now waits while Develop draws) and how much is `unload()`. **`unload()` alone showed no effect on SwiftShader:** in-page (`mem-unload.mjs`), after a 100 % render of the ARW the RSS stayed at 1152 MB whether or not the engine was unloaded (also with the device's queue ticked afterwards), and a second engine developing the same photo then reached 1726–1728 MB either way (peaks 2410–2424 MB). The software driver apparently doesn't give freed texture memory back to the process or to another device; the textures are destroyed, but what that saves on a real GPU wasn't measured.
- **Unit tests** (42 new, with the 4 above): `engine/gpu/guard.test.ts` (13: a fake device's validation, out-of-memory and internal errors, an `AbortError`, a loss during or after the work, a loss reported just after the failure, other errors passing through, balanced scopes, `DevelopEngine.guarded`, the retry rule, the up-front size check), `engine/gpu/present.test.ts` (6: one probe for concurrent and later calls, failure and exceptions, fallback adapters in both forms, `?gpu=`, no adapter), `engine/gpu/gate.test.ts` (6: idle, wait and quiet period, no starvation at the default and Export's limit, back-to-back frames, hold expiry, release on failure), `export/run.test.ts` (7: validation fails without a file or retry; a loss retries once on a fresh engine; out of memory and internal retry; a second loss fails; an engine lost before a photo is replaced first; other errors keep their message; each photo is unloaded), `develop/batch.test.ts` (6: retry while working out settings; retry of only the thumbnail after the save, one history step; no retry of validation; a second loss is a problem and the next photo goes on; "Settings applied" when only the thumbnail failed twice; unload per photo).
- From a clean `npm ci`: `npx tsc -b`, `npx oxlint` (the two existing warnings), `npx vitest run` (then 71 files, 680 tests; main `993f81b` had 65 and 638) and `npm run build` passed.

**Limitations and unverified:**
- **No real GPU was tried.** A real driver reset (`chrome://gpucrash`), a real out-of-memory error and a real present failure on hardware weren't produced; out of memory was stood in for by an invalid texture, and loss by `device.destroy()`. By the WebGPU spec out of memory takes the same invalid-object path.
- Whether other browsers reject `mapAsync` on a loss as Chromium does, and report `isFallbackAdapter`, wasn't checked.
- The probe can still be wrong where a loss is reported more than 50 ms after the probe's frame; `?gpu=` overrides it.
- Develop's own engine isn't guarded (error scopes would also catch the histogram's, loupe's and Auto's work): a validation or out-of-memory error during Develop's drawing still only reaches the console, as before. Develop's own read-backs (white balance picker, loupe, Auto) aren't guarded either.
- After a loss, Develop uploads the photo again from its kept decode; the decode stays in memory as long as the photo is shown (the engine already held the same data).
- A retry develops a photo twice; a photo whose thumbnail fails twice keeps its new settings with the old thumbnail.
- **The memory saving of `unload()` is unmeasured on a real GPU**, and on SwiftShader the GPU process kept its memory (above). The diagnosis's other memory ideas (reusing RCD temporaries, Bayer Stage A in strips, one shared device) weren't done.

## Part 15: Colour Mixer and Point Color

**What it does.** A **Colour Mixer** group after the Tone Curve and before Detail (jump bar: the colour wheel, "Colour"), with Lightroom's two tabs:
- **Mixer**: Lightroom's eight bands, Red, Orange, Yellow, Green, Aqua, Blue, Purple and Magenta, each with Hue, Saturation and Luminance from −100 to +100 (default 0), shown as **Hue · Saturation · Luminance · All** (8 or 24 sliders) or **Colour** (one band's three sliders, picked from 8 swatches). A targeted adjustment tool (drag on a colour in the photo), a readout of the bands under the pointer, Alt-drag on a slider (or **Visualize**) to see a band's colours with the rest grey, and darktable's settings under **Advanced**.
- **Point Color**: pick up to 8 colours on the photo; each has Hue, Saturation and Luminance Shift, Variance, Range (and Hue, Saturation and Luminance ranges) and **Visualize range**.

The engine is darktable's **color equalizer** (`src/iop/colorequal.c`, commit `6271f53`, GPL-3.0-or-later) translated to WebGPU: darktable UCS 22, per-hue tables, the saturation weight, its two guided filters with the Scharr halo guard, and gamut mapping, before the sigmoid. Point Color is LightCraft's design (MIT/Apache-2.0) moved to darktable UCS 22. Both are off by default: an edit without them is exactly an edit from before this part and renders byte for byte as before (below). A new tool that is off by default needs no new process version, so the current version stays 2.

This part is numbered 15: Part 14 is reserved for the GPU-robustness work on its own branch.

Owner's decisions (2026-10-13, after the research in [research/COLOUR_MIXER_EFFECTS.md](research/COLOUR_MIXER_EFFECTS.md), Part A):
1. **The Colour Mixer:** Lightroom's 8 bands × Hue/Saturation/Luminance, −100…+100, default 0, built on darktable's color equalizer (UCS 22, per-hue tables, saturation weighting, the guided filter for luminance with its halo guard, gamut mapping).
2. **Band positions like Lightroom's**, not darktable's 8 nodes 45° apart: LightCraft's guess (HSV 0, 30, 60, 120, 180, 225, 270, 315°), converted into UCS hue and documented. Lightroom's real centres aren't published.
3. **No spill:** a non-overshooting periodic interpolation between the bands instead of darktable's RBF, so one band at +100 never pushes distant hues the other way; its absence of overshoot unit-tested.
4. **Point Color in the first version**, as a tab of the mixer, in darktable UCS 22, with LightCraft's design as the reference.
5. **Defaults:** before the sigmoid, after Basic's Vibrance and Saturation; the research's scales (Hue ±100 ≈ ±30° of UCS hue; Saturation −100 grey, +100 about ×2; Luminance tuned by eye); guided filter on; a targeted adjustment tool with a hover marker, Alt-drag visualize and keyboard access as the Tone Curve's.
6. **Storage and Lightroom:** `colourMixer` in the develop settings, absent at defaults, no process version; Lightroom's `crs:HueAdjustment*`, `SaturationAdjustment*` and `LuminanceAdjustment*` read and written under Part 7's rules (closest match); Point Color's field only if confirmed.
7. **Plumbing:** Copy/Paste/Sync items in a Colour group (following the Tone Curve: ticked by default), presets with Amount, help text and group guidance, a jump-bar entry; the group after the Tone Curve and before Detail ([USABILITY.md](USABILITY.md) lists the Colour group there).

### Where it acts

| Behaviour | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Position | In the display shader (`develop-display.wgsl`, `developBeforeCurve`): white balance fix → exposure → tone equalizer → Vibrance/Saturation → **Colour Mixer → Point Color** → sigmoid → Tone Curve → output profile. On scene-referred linear Rec.2020. | Owner's decision 5; darktable's v5.0 order: colorequal 41.2, before sigmoid 45.3 (`src/common/iop_order.c` lines 336-388) |
| Colour space | darktable UCS 22 (`engine/color/ucs.ts`, the WGSL in `engine/gpu/colour-mixer.wgsl`): Rec.2020 → XYZ D65 → xyY → L* and U*′V*′ → JCH → HSB (hue, saturation S = C/B, brightness B). The Rec.2020 → XYZ matrix is LightBench's (D65); darktable goes through its ICC profile's D50 matrix and CAT16 (`colorequal.c` `process`, STEP 0). sRGB red reads 19.7° (darktable's comment: "sRGB primary red records at 20° of hue"). | darktable `src/common/colorspaces_inline_conversions.h` lines 1353-1528, `src/common/darktable_ucs_22_helpers.h` |
| Hue tables | Three 512-entry tables over [−180°, 180°) (darktable's `LUT_ELEM`; the research said 360): hue offset, saturation correction and brightness, plus the gamut boundary. Built on the CPU when a slider moves and uploaded as one 512 × 1 texture; read with darktable's `lookup_gamut` (linear, periodic). | `colorequal.c` `commit_params` 1864-1931; `src/common/math.h` `LUT_ELEM` |
| Off | No `colourMixer`, or one that changes nothing: the shader skips the stage (its uniform is all zero). | Part 1's rule |
| Everywhere | The tables and parameters travel in `displayUniforms` (`colourMixer`), so Develop at fit and 100 %, the histogram and Auto, the white balance loupe, thumbnails, culling previews, the background developer (Paste, Sync, presets) and Export all render it. | Part 1 |

### The bands

| Behaviour | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Names and order | Red, Orange, Yellow, Green, Aqua, Blue, Purple, Magenta: Lightroom's, in its order. darktable's are red, orange, yellow, green, cyan, blue, lavender, magenta. | darktable's Lightroom importer reads Lightroom's eight (`src/develop/lightroom.c` 621-764) |
| Centres | LightCraft's guess at Lightroom's centres, HSV hues 0, 30, 60, 120, 180, 225, 270, 315° of sRGB colours (`crates/develop/src/settings.rs` `MIXER_HUES`), converted to darktable UCS hue as the hue of the sRGB colour at that HSV hue with **HSV saturation 50 %** and value 100 %: **16.7, 54.3, 100.4, 138.1, 195.2, 271.6, 296.7, 331.1°** (gaps 37.6, 46.1, 37.7, 57.1, 76.4, 25.1, 34.3, 45.6°). Why 50 %: fully saturated sRGB colours lie on sRGB's gamut edge, where UCS hue bends: HSV 30° is UCS 36° at full saturation, 54° at 50 % and 61° at 25 %, and HSV 225° and 270° are only 12° apart at full saturation (277° and 289°). A middle saturation stands for photographs' colours. Checked on test colours (sRGB): a sky blue (0.35, 0.55, 0.85) reads 265° (Blue 98 %, Aqua 2 %), a skin tone (0.85, 0.62, 0.5) 41° (Orange 71 %, Red 29 %), grass (0.35, 0.55, 0.15) 127° (Green 81 %, Yellow 19 %). **Lightroom's own centres and widths aren't published**; these are a guess, not a match. | Owner's decision 2; LightBench's conversion (`colour-mixer.ts` `bandCentres`) |
| Falloff | **LightCraft's raised-cosine partition of unity** (`crates/pipeline/src/colorops.rs` `band_weights`, MIT/Apache-2.0, © the ArtCraft Team and the LightCraft contributors): between two neighbouring centres the nearer band's weight falls from 1 to 0 as ½ + ½ cos. Every hue belongs to at most two bands, the weights sum to 1, and each table entry is a weighted mean of two band values, so nothing overshoots: a band reaches exactly to its neighbours' centres. Blue at +100 alone moves the hues between Aqua's and Purple's centres (102°), by half or more over 51°, and nothing else. darktable's periodic RBF (`_periodic_RBF_interpolate` 1737-1791), recomputed here: one node at +1 dips to **−0.138, 61.6° from the node**, and is non-zero (> 0.05) over 150°. | Owner's decision 3 (either a periodic monotone spline or the raised cosine was allowed; the raised cosine was chosen because it is linear in the slider values: the targeted adjustment tool's and Visualize's band weights are then exactly what the tables do) |
| Node placement | darktable's "node placement" (±23°) turns all eight centres together (Advanced, "Band placement"). | `colorequal.c` line 145 |

### Slider scales (LightBench's; Lightroom's aren't public)

| Slider | Effect | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Hue ±100 | The band's hue offset ±30° of UCS hue (about to the neighbouring band). darktable: offset added to the pixel's hue. | Owner's decision 5 (the research's A.5) |
| Saturation ±100 | darktable's saturation correction 1 + v/200, applied as S × max(0, 1 + 2 (corr − 1)): **−100 is grey, +100 doubles S** (then gamut-limited). | `colorequal.c` `SAT_EFFECT` 2, STEP 5 |
| Luminance ±100 | darktable's brightness table 1 + v/100, i.e. **darktable's full brightness slider (0 … 200 %)**: b = S × (table − 1), then B × (1 + 8 b) for brightening (darktable's `BRIGHT_EFFECT` 8). **LightBench's change: darkening uses the reciprocal, B / (1 − 8 b)**, so −100 never reaches black (darktable's linear gain clamps to 0 for S > 0.125). The change scales with saturation: greys never change; at ±100 a pale sky (sRGB 0.6, 0.72, 0.9; S 0.05) moves by ×1.41 or ÷1.41 in B, a sky blue (S 0.14) by ×2.1 or ÷2.1. Tuned by eye on the D700, D80/JPEG landscape and Sony ILCE-7M2 test photos (Blue −100 deepens a pale sky clearly, Orange +100 lifts autumn leaves, all Saturation −100 gives a grey picture); the scale was kept at darktable's own range. | Owner's decision 5 ("tuned by eye"); `colorequal.c` 1119-1124, 1166-1170 |

### darktable's color equalizer, as translated

| Step | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Saturation weight | A logistic of the pixel's RGB saturation (max − min) / max, blurred: 1 / (1 + exp(−(30 + 20 contrast) (sat − shift))), shift = −0.015 + 0.3 (5 threshold)² (0.06 at the default threshold 0.1, "an inflection point at 6 %"); brightness uses shift + 0.01 × max brightness. Computed directly instead of darktable's 8193-entry table. | `_init_satweights` 470-481, `_get_satweight`, `commit_params`, `_prepare_process` 929-973 |
| Chroma prefilter | A 2-channel guided filter of U*′V*′ by itself (σ = 0.75 px at full size, ε 1e-5), blended back by the saturation weight. | `_prefilter_chromaticity` 611-714, `_apply_prefilter` |
| Corrections | At the prefiltered hue: hue offset, saturation correction, b = S × (brightness − 1); none for chroma ≤ 2⁻¹⁶. | STEP 3, 1080-1130 |
| Halo guard | The Scharr gradient of the blurred saturation, gain 4 √(max brightness) × scale², squared above 0.02, blurred (σ = 1 px). | 1103-1110, 921-924 |
| Guided filter | Saturation and brightness corrections guided by the prefiltered U*′V*′ (σ 0.5 px, ε 1e-6; brightness corrections blurred with 0.1 σ), then the saturation correction faded by the saturation weight and the brightness correction by (1 − gradient) × the brightness weight. Hue offsets aren't filtered (darktable's neither). | `_guide_with_chromaticity` 716-927 |
| Singular covariance | darktable treats a regularised covariance with |det| ≤ 4 FLT_EPSILON as singular (a = 0, the corrections' local mean). With UCS chromaticities of a few hundredths, that is most of a photo except strong colour edges, so in flat colour the filter is a Gaussian mean of the corrections. Kept as darktable has it. | 571, 870 |
| Radii | darktable's defaults (hue analysis radius 1.5 px, effect radius 1 px), scaled with the rendering: σ = 0.75 s, 0.5 s (at least 0.2 px), saturation and gradient σ = max(0.5, s), for s target pixels per photo pixel (1 for Export and 100 % on a 1× screen; capped at 2). Gaussians sampled and cut at 3 σ (darktable: recursive filters). | `_prepare_process`; LightBench's sampling |
| Gamut | darktable's `gamut_map_HSB` (saturation soft-clipped between 80 % and 100 % of Rec.2020's boundary at the colour's hue and lightness), from darktable's gamut table built for Rec.2020. **LightBench's change:** only the compression a change adds is applied (what the map would do to the pixel as it came in is subtracted), and never past the mapped value or the starting saturation. A pixel the mixer doesn't change stays exactly as it was; a colour that starts inside 80 % of the boundary gets darktable's map; the result is continuous in the change. darktable's map takes the boundary at the lightness before clipping, so a result can end a little outside: on 2000 random Rec.2020 colours with every Saturation at +100, up to 6.6 % past darktable's boundary and 4.4 % below zero in one channel, both next to Rec.2020's green primary. | `darktable_ucs_22_helpers.h` `gamut_map_HSB`, `dt_UCS_22_build_gamut_LUT` |
| Output chroma | **LightBench's change:** the pixel keeps its own chromaticity; the prefiltered one only steers its corrections. darktable writes the prefiltered chromaticity out, which smooths chroma wherever the module is on (a sub-pixel chroma blur on every saturated pixel). | STEP 3 (`dt_UCS_LUV_to_JCH` on the filtered UV) |
| Not offered | darktable's "hue curve" (the RBF's width; there is no RBF) and the two radii (fixed at darktable's defaults; larger radii would need darktable's down-scaled filtering). Threshold, contrast, white level, node placement and the guided filter's on/off are under Advanced. | LightBench |

### Point Color

| Behaviour | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Picking | **Pick** (or the command "Point Color: pick a colour on the photo"), then click the photo: the 3 × 3 pixels there, as they reach Point Color (after the bands, before the points), become a sample (`engine.sampleMixer`). It is selected and the picker ends; Esc cancels. Up to 8 samples, applied in order (later ones act on what earlier ones changed). | LightCraft's design and limit of 8 ("Lightroom's limit" in its code, unconfirmed); Lightroom's order of samples [search] |
| Stored | The sample's darktable UCS hue (°), saturation S and brightness B, the four shifts (−100…100) and four ranges (0…100, default 50). | LightBench |
| Range | LightCraft's soft box (`PointK`), in UCS: hue within (0.1 + 0.6 hue range) × k radians, saturation within (0.01 + 0.06 sat range) × k, lightness J within (0.05 + 0.4 lum range) × k, each with a smoothstep edge from half the width; k = max(0.05, Range / 50). Hue is ignored for a near-neutral sample (S < 0.01), which then selects greys by lightness and saturation; chromatic samples fade out towards grey (S 0.005…0.025). The hue and lightness widths are LightCraft's numbers; the saturation widths are LightCraft's halved, because UCS saturation runs about half OkLCh chroma on photographs' colours. Tuned by eye. | LightCraft `colorops.rs` 63-126; LightBench's scaling |
| Shifts | Hue Shift ±100 = ±30° (as the bands); Saturation Shift −100 grey, +100 doubles S; Luminance Shift ±100 = B × 2^±0.5, about ±1 EV in the mid-tones (UCS brightness follows lightness, about Y^0.49 at middle grey); each weighted by the colour's place in the range. | LightBench (LightCraft: ±0.5 rad, chroma × 0…2, ±0.2 OkLab L) |
| Variance | −100 pulls the colours in the range 80 % of the way towards the sample in hue, saturation and log brightness; +100 pushes them apart (LightCraft's reading: "colour contrast within the range"). Adobe's help summary calls Variance "how far the adjustment spreads to similar colours" [search]; which is right wasn't checked. | LightCraft; **unverified against Lightroom** |
| Visualize range | The selected sample's range in colour (as the colours reach Point Color), the rest grey. | Lightroom's Visualize Range [search] |
| Gamut | After the points, the same gamut map as the bands. | LightBench |

### The Colour group (`develop/ColourMixerPanel.tsx`)

| Behaviour | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Tabs and views | **Mixer** · **Point Color**; in Mixer, **Hue · Saturation · Luminance · All · Colour** (a dot marks a channel with a moved slider). Slider tracks are painted with the band's colours (hue −30° … +30°, grey … saturated, dark … light; darktable draws its sliders' gradients the same way, `_draw_sliders_*_gradient`). The view is remembered in this browser. | Lightroom Classic's HSL tabs and Color view [memory]; darktable |
| Hover marker | With the targeted adjustment tool on, the panel reads "Under the pointer: Blue 98 %, Aqua 2 %" and marks those bands' sliders (and swatches) by their weight; a grey reads "grey (the mixer leaves it alone)". | LightBench's idea (the research's A.4) |
| Targeted adjustment | The target button, **Ctrl+Alt+Shift+H / S / L** (Hue, Saturation, Luminance; the view switches to that channel), Ctrl+Alt+Shift+N, Esc or Enter end it. Dragging up or down on a colour moves its bands' sliders of that channel in proportion to their weights, the main band by the full amount (200 px = 100; Shift a quarter), one history step "Colour mixer: Blue Luminance (targeted)"; ↑ ↓ while hovering (Shift: ×5) nudge them, merged into one step. Alt held during the drag shows the main band's colours. It has its own keyboard context, "Develop: colour mixer tool" (`mixer`), shared with Point Color's picker; Undo/Redo, \\, J and Z still work. Starting it ends the crop tool, Guided Upright, the Remove brush, the Tone Curve's tool and the white balance picker, and the reverse. | Lightroom's TAT ("moves the appropriate sliders" [search]); the keys are Lightroom Classic's **as remembered, not checked** (research A.1); LightBench's proportion rule |
| Visualize | Alt-drag (Option on a Mac) on a band slider shows that band's colours and greys the rest while dragging, as Lightroom's Alt-drag [search]; the **Visualize** checkbox does the same for the band last moved (or the Colour view's band), for desktops that take Alt-drag. | Lightroom [search]; the Detail group's Show mask precedent |
| Advanced | Use guided filter (on), Saturation threshold (10 %), Threshold contrast (0 %), White level (+1 EV, −2…+2), Band placement (0°), each with darktable's name in its help, and a note that Lightroom's band centres aren't published. | darktable's module parameters |
| Reset, eye, history | **Reset** (the 24 sliders), **Reset Hue/Saturation/Luminance**, **Reset colour** and **Remove** / **Remove all** for points, **Reset Advanced**; the group's eye hides the mixer and Point Color ("Hidden: Colour Mixer"). Sliders merge like Basic's; drags, picks and resets are their own steps. | Parts 1 and 12 |
| Guidance | Under the title: "One colour at a time: skies, foliage, skin. Basic first; then single colours." Its tooltip says what the two tabs do, to set white balance and Basic's Vibrance/Saturation first, and that greys aren't changed. Every slider has a hint and a tooltip (`help.ts`: `mixerHue`, `mixerSaturation`, `mixerLuminance`, `point*`); band sliders show the hint only in the Colour view, where the three differ. | Owner's rule (USABILITY.md) |

### Copy, paste, presets and the stored edit

| Behaviour | Detail |
|---|---|
| Stored | `colourMixer` in the develop settings and `lightbench:Develop`: `hue`, `saturation`, `luminance` (8 integers each, Lightroom's band order), `points` (when any) and `advanced` (only when it differs from darktable's defaults). **Absent when untouched or reset**: `normalizeSettings` and `withColourMixer` drop a mixer with no band moved, no point and default Advanced settings, so such an edit's JSON is exactly an old edit's. |
| Copy, Sync, Paste, New preset | A **Colour** group with two items, **Colour Mixer** (the bands and Advanced) and **Point Color**, **ticked by default** as the Tone Curve's item; each keeps the target's other part. A photo without a mixer copies "none". Auto Sync sends only what changed. |
| Presets and Amount | A preset can hold both. The Amount moves each band from the photo's value to the preset's (0 % as it was, 200 % twice the change); the preset's points come with their shifts and Variance scaled the same way (from the photo's point at the same place, or 0); Advanced follows the preset above 0 % (`blendColourMixer`). |
| Process version | None: a new tool, off by default; no existing rendering changes (byte-identical, below). A later change to its maths, band positions or scales needs a new version. |

### Lightroom's HSL in sidecars (`sidecar/lightroom.ts`)

| Behaviour | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Fields | `crs:HueAdjustment<Band>`, `SaturationAdjustment<Band>`, `LuminanceAdjustment<Band>` for Red, Orange, Yellow, Green, Aqua, Blue, Purple, Magenta. | darktable `lightroom.c` 621-764; LightCraft `docs/xmp-interop.md` |
| Mapping | Number for number (both are −100…100 sliders with the same names). The look is only approximately Lightroom's: band centres, widths and scales differ and Lightroom's aren't public (closest match, owner's decision 6). A sidecar with none of the 24 fields leaves the mixer alone; absent fields read 0. Reading keeps the photo's Point Color and Advanced settings. | Owner's decision 6 |
| Reading and writing | Part 7's rules, as the Detail fields (Part 13): read when a photo is added (the "Lightroom settings" step) and by **Read changes**; written only into a sidecar that already has `crs:ProcessVersion` and isn't already applied, only the fields LightBench changed since the file was last written, each in place; integers, positive ones with a sign ("+13", as LightCraft's hand-written test sidecar has them). The HSL numbers are part of the `lightbench:LightroomGeometry` marker; markers and change records from before this part have none and never count as a difference. | Part 7; the sign **not checked against a sidecar Lightroom wrote** |
| Point Color | **Not read or written.** `crs:PointColors` is named only by LightCraft's code and tests (an "empty" slot written as four −1 values, `crates/engine/src/crs.rs` 493, 850-855); its format isn't documented anywhere available here and LightCraft doesn't import it either. | Owner's decision 6 ("only if confirmed") |
| B&W mix | `crs:GrayMixer<Band>` isn't mapped (LightBench has no B&W treatment yet). | Research A.7 |

### Engine (`engine/gpu/develop-engine.ts`, `colour-mixer.wgsl`, `colour-mixer-filter.wgsl`)

| Step | Detail |
|---|---|
| Per pixel | `mixApplyPixel` in the display shader: the pixel's UCS HSB, its band corrections (from the tables, or from the guided filter), the points, the gamut map, back to Rec.2020. A pixel whose corrections are exactly neutral and that no point touches is returned unchanged, without a round trip through the colour space; in flat neutral regions the filter's arithmetic gives exactly neutral corrections (weighted means of constants), so pixels away from a moved band stay bit-identical (GPU check below). |
| Guided filter | Every drawing (canvas, histogram, thumbnail, loupe, export, the pickers) is made in tiles of 1024 × 1024 target pixels when the guided filter runs: the mixer's input around the tile (U*′, V*′, RGB saturation, L*; the drawn image's edge repeated beyond it, as darktable's module sees only the cropped photo) is drawn into a tile texture with the filters' combined reach as margin (7 px for a fitted view, 12 px at 100 %, 21 px at 2×), four compute passes run darktable's filters, and the tile is drawn with their corrections. Five rgba32float tile textures (88 MB at 100 %, 1048 × 1048 each); no full-size buffer, so a 24 MP export takes 24 tiles. The filter runs on every redraw, because its corrections depend on the sliders and the exposure. |
| Bindings | The display shader got bindings 7 (corrections), 8 (the tile), 9 (the Mixer uniform, 544 bytes) and 10 (the tables). `View` is unchanged (512 bytes). The Tone Curve's binding 6, the tone mask's 5 and the lens's 4 are untouched. |
| CPU twin | `colour-mixer.ts` (`mixImage`, `guidedCorrections`, `mixPixel`) repeats the shader's arithmetic in double precision, the image's edge repeated by the same margin. `engine.mixerImages` reads the GPU's input and output (linear Rec.2020 before the sigmoid) for the comparison. |
| GPU robustness (Part 14) | The tile textures and the filter's parameter buffer belong to one drawing: freed once it is submitted, and also when encoding it fails part-way (`encodeDisplay`). `unload()` frees the hue-table texture, clears what the last drawing bound and zeroes the Mixer uniform, so the next photo starts with the mixer off. A new engine after a lost device makes the tables and the filter's pipelines again on its first drawing (nothing of the mixer's is kept outside the engine). The pre-pass is part of `render`, `renderOutput` and `renderThumbnail`, so in Export, the background developer and the Library's 100 % view it runs inside `guarded()` and after the foreground gate; Develop's mixer readings (the hover readout, Point Color's picker) hold the gate like its drawings. |
| Benchmark (Part 16) | Two rows after the tone mask: the mixer with the guided filter on (its input draw, labelled `mixerInput`, the four filter passes and the display pass, each with a ↳ row) and off. See Part 16. |

Test files: Nikon D700 `philadelphia.nef`, Nikon D80 `glarus.nef`, Sony ILCE-6300 and ILCE-7M2 ARWs (immich test assets), `test-srgb.jpg`.

The engine's own mixer input and output (`engine.mixerImages`: linear Rec.2020 before the sigmoid) against the CPU twin (`mixImage`) run on the GPU's input, for five mixers on the JPEG and the D80: bands with the guided filter (Hue, Saturation and Luminance on six bands), the same without the filter, Blue Saturation +100 alone, two Point Color samples picked from the photo (with shifts, Variance and a wider range) alone, and bands plus points.
  - At 300 px (scale 0.19 and 0.08, one tile): largest relative difference 1.2·10⁻⁴, mean 2.1–4.9·10⁻⁵, no pixel over 1 %.

- **Unit tests** (`npx vitest run`: 724 tests in 73 files; main 688 in 69):
  - `engine/color/ucs.test.ts` (4): D65 at the origin, sRGB red at 19.7° (darktable: 20°); L* and HSB round trips (500 random colours, < 10⁻⁹); Rec.2020's gamut table puts its primaries and secondaries on the boundary within 6 %; the soft clip between 80 % and 100 %, never past it.
  - `engine/develop/colour-mixer.test.ts` (19): the band centres (to 0.1°) and node placement; raised-cosine weights (3600 hues: at most two bands, summing to 1); **no overshoot**: every band at ±100 alone, each channel, moves only the hues between its neighbours' centres and only in the slider's direction, and 500 random mixers keep every table between their band values; darktable's own RBF does overshoot (one node at +1 dips below −0.1); **continuity across 0°/360° and at the tables' ends (±180°)**; **greys untouched** with extreme sliders, with and without the guided filter, through a whole grey image and with a coloured Point Color sample (a grey sample does move greys by lightness, by design); nearly grey colours change at most by 8 S in brightness; the slider scales at each band's centre; darkening never reaches black; **gamut**: 2000 random Rec.2020 colours with every Saturation at +100 never lose saturation, end at most 6.6 % past the boundary and never more than 5 % below zero in a channel; Point Color at its sample, far from it and with Variance; the guided filter's margin (changing pixels farther than the margin away changes nothing, at scales 0.3, 1 and 2); no spill from the filter (a red/green picture with Red moved: the green half beyond the margin is bit-identical); darktable's radii; **storage absent at defaults** (no key, the JSON of an old edit unchanged, reset removes the key, an unchanged mixer gives back the same object); clamping and rounding; preset Amount; the Mixer uniform's layout against the WGSL struct.
  - `sidecar/lightroom-hsl.test.ts` (7): the 24 field names; reading (signed and unsigned) number for number, absent fields 0; no HSL fields = unknown, not a difference; taking Lightroom's bands keeps Point Color and Advanced; old markers without HSL compare equal; writing only changed fields, signed, in place, never into a sidecar without HSL fields or without Camera Raw settings; Lightroom's change noticed.

  - `settings/keymap.test.ts` (+1): Ctrl+Alt+Shift+H/S/L in Develop and the mixer context, N ending it, ↑ ↓, Esc, Enter, Undo, \\ in the tool's context and not the next photo or the grid; no conflicts.
  - `npx tsc -b`, `npx oxlint` (the two existing warnings), `npm run build` passed after `npm ci`.

  - The earlier run's two failures were the test's: its Alt-drag check sets the Blue slider by clicking it (to 46), so the copy description and the preset's half no longer matched the fixed 40 and 20 it expected; the checks now read the values from the photo's own settings.
  - Screenshots checked by eye: the panel's Saturation, Luminance (painted tracks), Colour and Point Color views, the hover readout, the copy dialog, the Alt-drag view, the Visualize range view.
- **Tuning by eye** (`looks.mjs`, 640 px renders): Blue Luminance −100 deepens the D700's pale sky clearly without touching the fountain's white water; Green Luminance −100 darkens its foliage a little (low saturation); Orange Luminance +100 lifts the ILCE-7M2's autumn leaves; every Saturation at −100 turns it grey; on the JPEG landscape Blue Saturation +60 / Luminance −60 deepens the sky and the blue haze while the clouds stay white.
- **Speed** (SwiftShader, keypress to drawn frame, median of 6 steps, 890 × 662 canvas, one tile; noisy: two runs of the same script disagreed by up to 3×):

  | | Run 1 | Run 2 |
  |---|---|---|
  | Vibrance, no mixer | 2.97 s | 4.27 s |
  | Red Saturation, guided filter on | 11.4 s | 7.36 s |
  | Red Saturation, guided filter off | 4.35 s | 1.54 s |
  | Vibrance with the mixer (guided) on | 12.5 s | 4.18 s |

  With the filter the mixer's input is drawn as well as the picture and four compute passes run, every redraw. **Real GPUs were not measured.** Estimate: a fitted view of about 0.6 M pixels needs one extra pass of the display shader and about 100 texture reads per pixel in the filter (60 M reads), well under a few milliseconds on a discrete GPU; a 24 MP export adds 24 tiles of the same.

### Limitations and unverified

- **Lightroom.** Nothing was checked in Lightroom. Its band centres, widths, slider scales and pipeline position aren't public: the bands are LightCraft's guess converted to UCS, the scales LightBench's, so the same numbers look only roughly like Lightroom's. The TAT keys (Ctrl+Alt+Shift+H/S/L/N) are remembered, not checked. Point Color's Variance follows LightCraft's reading; Adobe's description differs [search]. The limit of 8 samples is LightCraft's claim. The HSL sidecar fields were tested with a sidecar written for the test; the "+13" sign format isn't confirmed from a file Lightroom wrote. Point Color isn't exchanged with Lightroom (`crs:PointColors` undocumented).
- **darktable differences (all LightBench's choices, above):** band centres; the raised-cosine tables; darkening by the reciprocal gain; the output keeps its own chromaticity; the gamut map applies only added compression; sampled Gaussians cut at 3 σ; fixed radii; Rec.2020's matrix without darktable's D50/CAT16 round trip. Not compared with darktable's own output (darktable's colour equalizer with LightBench's band centres isn't something darktable can render).
- **Magnified drawings:** above 2 target pixels per photo pixel (100 % on a screen denser than 2×) the radii stay at 2×'s, so the filter is slightly narrower there than at Export.
- **Gamut:** darktable's single-step map can leave very saturated colours (past 95 % of Rec.2020's boundary, next to its primaries) a few % outside Rec.2020, as darktable's does; the sigmoid's negative-value handling takes it from there.
- **Speed:** with the guided filter, each redraw draws the mixer's input as well as the picture and runs four compute passes (SwiftShader: between 1 × and 3.8 × the time of a Vibrance step without the mixer in two noisy runs, see Verified; real GPUs not measured).
- Hover readout and Point Color's picker read 3 × 3 rendered pixels per pointer move, like the Tone Curve's.


## Part 16: GPU benchmark

**What it does.** A **GPU benchmark** section at the foot of the System Check page times each step of LightBench's develop pipeline on the computer's own graphics card, with test images made in the page, and gives a table to copy and paste back. Every speed number in Parts 1–14 came from SwiftShader, a software renderer running on the CPU; this is how the owner's RTX 4000 Ada (or any other GPU) gets real numbers. It is IMPROVEMENTS.md item C20a (the real-GPU benchmark harness), without C20a's fixed set of real photos and without a release gate yet. Nothing renders differently: the benchmark adds code paths and changes no existing one (engine-level identity below).

### How to run it

1. Open LightBench in **Chrome or Edge**, go to **System Check** (top bar), and scroll to **GPU benchmark**. Close other apps that use the graphics card (games, video calls, other LightBench tabs), and keep the laptop on mains power.
2. Check **Graphics adapter**: on a laptop with two GPUs it should name the NVIDIA card. LightBench asks for the high-performance adapter, but Windows can still hand the browser the integrated one; if so, set Chrome or Edge to "High performance" in Windows Settings → System → Display → Graphics, restart the browser and reload the page.
3. Press **Run Quick** (about 30 seconds on a discrete GPU) or **Run Full** (a few minutes). A progress bar shows the stage and run; **Cancel** stops after the current run and keeps what was measured. Leaving the page also cancels.
4. Press **Copy results (Markdown)** (or **JSON**) and paste the text into the conversation. **Show the results as text** shows the same Markdown, for copying by hand if the browser refuses the clipboard.

Optionally, **Also use a photo from my library** adds one photo of the library to the run (the stages of the test image of its kind). It is read locally from its folder, which must be connected in this session; its name, camera and pixels are not in the results, only e.g. "Your photo (Bayer RAW, 24.2 MP)".

Nothing is sent anywhere. The results hold the app version and commit, the date, the browser's user agent, the adapter's vendor, architecture, device and description strings, whether it is a fallback (software) adapter, whether it has `timestamp-query` and `shader-f16`, eight of its limits, the screen's devicePixelRatio, the canvas mode and why it was chosen, and the measurements. No serial numbers or other identifiers.

### Test images

Made in the page and deterministic (`bench/synthetic.ts`), so nothing is downloaded and every machine times the same pixels. One procedural scene (gradients, a colour-checker-like set of patches, fine chirps that stress the demosaic, a highlight above the sensor's white so highlight reconstruction has work, shot and read noise) is written as:

| Image | Size | Posing as |
|---|---|---|
| Bayer 24 MP | 6000 × 4000, RGGB, 14 bits, black 512 | a Sony ILCE-6300 at ISO 400 (so noise reduction uses darktable's real profile for that camera), with Sony-style lens data (distortion, lateral CA and vignetting knots, the kind the A6300 test file carries), so the lens correction needs no Lensfun download |
| Bayer 45 MP (Full) | 8256 × 5504 | the same |
| X-Trans 26 MP (Full) | 6240 × 4160, the X-T4's pattern | a Fujifilm X-T4 at ISO 400, no lens data |
| sRGB 24 MP | 6000 × 4000, 8 bits | a JPEG's decoded pixels with the sRGB profile |

Each image goes through `loadIntoEngine` (develop/source.ts) like a decoded photo, then through the stages with its kind's default edit (`defaultsFor`: for the RAWs, sharpening Amount 40 and lens correction on), changed per stage as listed below. For testing on software renderers, `?benchmp=N` scales the images to N megapixels; the page, the image labels ("Bayer 0.30 MP (synthetic, scaled)") and the results then say so, because such numbers can't be compared with the nominal sizes.

### What each number means

Quick runs the 24 MP Bayer image through every stage below and the sRGB image through Stage A, the fitted display pass and the export render, with 1 warm-up and 3 measured runs per stage. Full adds the 45 MP Bayer image (every stage), the X-Trans image (Stage A with Markesteijn 1- and 3-pass, display at fit and 100 %, both export rows) and more sRGB stages, with 2 warm-ups and 5 runs.

| Row | What runs (the engine's own API) | What each run changes, so the work is done again |
|---|---|---|
| **Stage A: RAW → scene** | `engine.process(stageOptions(...))`: rawprepare, highlights (inpaint opposed, including one GPU → CPU read-back of its chroma statistics), demosaic, the scene's mip chain. For the JPEG: the input stage (profile → linear, inverse tone mapping, mips). | nothing: every call builds the scene again |
| ↳ rawprepare, highlights, demosaic (RCD; Markesteijn for X-Trans), mip chain | the same runs' passes, grouped by entry point (`prepare`; `hl*`; `rcd*` and `writeRcd`, or `xt*`; `mip`). GPU time only, so only with timestamp-query. | — |
| Stage A, Markesteijn 3-pass (Full, X-Trans) | the same with the 3-pass method | — |
| **Geometry warp: lens correction + straighten** | `renderStats(…, 64)` with lens correction on and a 0.5° angle: the full-size bicubic warp through the combined map (`warp.wgsl` + `lens.wgsl`) and its mip chain; GPU = the `warp` and `mip` passes. No Detail. | the angle, by 0.001° |
| **Tone equalizer mask pre-pass** | `renderStats(…, 64)` with process version 2 and Shadows +40: the full-size guided-filter statistics; GPU = `moments`, `reduce`, `blurX`, `blurY` | the red white-balance multiplier, by 0.01 % (the mask is keyed on it) |
| **Colour Mixer**, guided filter on and off (Part 15) | `render(…, fit)` with a mixer on (Green Hue +15, Orange Luminance +20, Blue Saturation +40 and Luminance −20), one redraw as in the display rows. Guided filter on: the mixer's input drawn around each 1024 px tile (`mixerInput`), darktable's two guided filters (`prefilterStats`, `prefilterApply`, `guideStats`, `guideApply`) and the display pass with their corrections, each with a ↳ row; GPU = all of them. Off (Advanced → guided filter off): the display pass alone, the bands read from the tables per pixel. The filter isn't cached, so every redraw pays for it; compare with "Display pass, fit, without Tone Curve". Quick: the Bayer image; Full: the sRGB image too. | Exposure, by 0.01 EV |
| **Colour Grading on: display pass, fit** (Part 17) | `render(…, fit)` with a grade on (all four wheels tinted, two with luminance), one redraw: the display pass alone, which runs darktable's color balance rgb twice per pixel (the grade and the neutral run it subtracts); GPU = the display pass. Compare with "Display pass, fit, without Tone Curve". Quick: the Bayer image; Full: the sRGB image too. | Exposure, by 0.01 EV |
| **Detail: denoise (profiled, wavelets)** | `render(…, 100 %)` with Luminance 30, Color 25 and no sharpening: the full-size wavelet denoise (7 scales) and its mip chain, then one redraw; GPU = the denoise passes and mips | Luminance 30 → 31 → 32 |
| **Detail: capture sharpening, Amount 40** | the same with sharpening only (8 Richardson–Lucy iterations): masks, iterations, result; GPU = the `cs*` passes and mips. **Only the shipped separable kernel is measured: the 9 × 9 kernel it replaced (Part 13) is no longer in the code.** | Detail 25 → 26 → 27 |
| **Display pass**, fit and 100 %, without and with a Tone Curve | `render(…)` on the benchmark's canvas (full width of the section, `min(50vh, 420px)` high; its size in device pixels is in the results) after an Exposure change of 0.01 EV, with everything upstream cached: one slider redraw; GPU = the display pass. In compatibility (read-back) mode the wall time includes copying the frame back and drawing it on a 2D canvas. | Exposure |
| **Remove: one spot** | `renderStats(…, 64)` with one Remove spot (radius 1 % of the short side, feather 0.5), no Detail, no lens: reading the scene's tiles, the fill search (on the CPU, in the retouch worker), writing the retouched copy and its mips | the spot's position |
| **Export render** | `renderOutput` at full size, 8-bit sRGB, as Export calls it (no output sharpening, Export's default), read back; Detail, warp and masks already cached, as when exporting right after editing | Exposure |
| **Export end to end** | `process` then `renderOutput`: Stage A, Detail (Amount 40, full size), the lens warp of the detailed scene and the render: one exported photo's GPU work after decoding | nothing (Stage A drops every cache) |
| **JPEG encode** | `encodeJpeg` (jpegli, WebAssembly in a worker) of the last export render at quality 90 and 4:4:4 (Export's defaults), without metadata. CPU only. | — |
| **Thumbnail** | `renderThumbnail` (a 512 px render and the browser's JPEG encoder), as the background developer refreshes one; Detail at the thumbnail's level is cached after the warm-up | Exposure |

The **Colour Mixer** rows (Part 15) sit after the tone mask, where `STAGES` in `bench/run.ts` (in pipeline order) kept a place for them, and the **Colour Grading** row (Part 17) after them.

The columns:
- **Median / Min**: wall-clock time of the engine call, from just before it until `queue.onSubmittedWorkDone()` resolves after it (`performance.now()`): what a person waits for, CPU work and read-backs included.
- **GPU median / GPU min**: the sum of the stage's passes from **timestamp queries**, where the adapter has `timestamp-query`. Chrome rounds them to 0.1 ms unless its WebGPU developer features are on (`chrome://flags/#enable-webgpu-developer-features`); the results say when the values look rounded, and a stage of many tiny passes is then off by up to 0.1 ms per pass. Without timestamp-query (or with `?benchts=0`) the GPU columns and the ↳ rows are empty and the results say "wall-clock only".
- **Peak memory**: the most bytes of buffers and textures the benchmark's engine had allocated during the stage, warm-ups included. LightBench has no memory accounting of its own, so this is **estimated from the allocations**: each buffer's size and each texture's texels × bytes per texel over its mip levels, from creation until it is destroyed. Driver padding, the canvas's own textures and the browser's overhead are not included. Per image the results also give the peak over all its stages and what stayed allocated after `unload()`.
- Median and minimum are over the measured runs only. The warm-ups (shader compilation, the noise profiles' download, the JPEG encoder's WebAssembly, first-use caches) are not counted.

### How it measures

| Part | Detail |
|---|---|
| Its own engine | `DevelopEngine.create('auto', profiler)`: the page's canvas mode (Part 14's probe, or `?gpu=`), and a `DeviceInstrument` (new, optional) that asks for `timestamp-query` where the adapter has it and sees the device before the engine makes anything on it. Engines made without one (Develop, Export, the background developer, the 100 % view) are made exactly as before. |
| Pass times (`bench/profiler.ts`) | On that device only, every compute and render pass begun while a run is measured gets `timestampWrites`, and is named after the pipeline it sets: the compute entry point, or the render pipeline's label (the engine's render pipelines now have labels: `display`, `develop`, `mip`, and the Colour Mixer's input draw `mixerInput` (Part 15); labels change no pixels). After the run the queries are resolved and read back. Up to 8,192 passes per run are timed. |
| Statistics (`bench/stats.ts`) | Median (the mean of the middle two for an even count) and minimum of the finite values. |
| Never with Develop | Before every run it waits until the foreground gate (Part 14) is quiet, and before every stage until the background developer (Paste, Sync, presets) has nothing running or queued. The System Check page never shows Develop at the same time. |
| Device loss | Each stage (its warm-ups and runs) is one `guarded` call (Part 14). A lost device, out of memory or an internal error runs the stage once more on a new engine with the image loaded again; the row is marked "(recovered)" and its note says why. A validation error, or a second failure, marks the row "failed" with the message, and the benchmark goes on with the next stage (on a new engine if the device is gone). |
| Lazy | `SystemCheck.tsx` loads `bench/Benchmark.tsx` with `React.lazy`: the benchmark is its own 29.8 kB chunk (12.1 kB gzipped) plus 1.0 kB of CSS, loaded only when System Check opens. |
| Test switches | `?benchmp=N` (scaled test images) and `?benchts=0` (wall-clock only, to test that path or to sidestep a driver's bad timestamps) in every build; `?benchlose=<stage id>` (lose the device once before that stage, to test recovery) on the development server only. |

### Verified

Headless Chromium 141 with SwiftShader WebGPU (a fallback adapter, which **has** `timestamp-query`), the Vite development server, `?gpu=readback&benchmp=0.3` (the test images at 0.3 MP: at nominal size the denoise alone takes minutes per run on SwiftShader). Scripts in the scratchpad's `gpubench/` folder.
- **Quick, end to end** (`e2e-bench.mjs`): the adapter facts (google · swiftshader, fallback yes, timestamp-query available, canvas mode "readback (forced)"); Cancel offered while running; 17 stage rows and 6 ↳ rows, none failed, every stage row with a median; **Copy results** put on the clipboard Markdown with the title, app version and commit, date, user agent, adapter, features and limits, both timing methods, the scaling note, one table row per row on the page and no file names, and JSON with the same rows and each image's memory; the System Check report gained a GPU benchmark section; no page errors. A second run cancelled after 6 s stopped with "Cancelled" and partial results, and the buttons came back.
- **With the Colour Mixer rows** (Part 15; `cm/bench/e2e-bench-mixer.mjs`, a copy of `e2e-bench.mjs` expecting 19 stage rows and checking the mixer's): Quick, **37 of 37**: 19 stage rows and 9 ↳ rows, none failed; the two mixer rows right after the tone mask and before denoise, both timed, with the three ↳ rows. SwiftShader at 0.3 MP, canvas 938 × 420 (**says nothing about a real GPU**): guided filter on 2843 ms median (GPU 2805 ms: input 932 ms, filters 1083 ms, display pass 813 ms; peak 49 MB with the five tile textures), off 918 ms (GPU 909 ms; 16 MB), "Display pass, fit, without Tone Curve" 966 ms in the same run. After each image's `unload()` 34 kB stayed allocated, the same after the Bayer image (which ran the mixer) as after the sRGB image (which didn't).
- **Wall-clock only** (`?benchts=0`): every GPU cell empty, every stage row still timed, the Markdown says "unavailable (no timestamp-query): wall-clock only" and the ↳ rows "needs timestamp-query".
- **Recovery** (`?benchlose=export`): the device was lost before the export stage's first run; the stage ran again on a new engine (marked "(recovered)", the loss logged as a warning) and every later stage ran on it, none failed.

A SwiftShader sample (Quick, test images at 0.3 MP, the canvas 938 × 420; **it says nothing about a real GPU**, it shows what the table looks like; the ↳ rows are medians of the parts, so they don't add up exactly to the median above them):

- **Rendering unchanged**: engine level, Part 14's `identity.mjs` run on this branch for 9 of its 19 cases (A.NEF defaults with the RAW sharpening, spots with crop, angle and custom white balance, a Tone Curve with crop and angle, Detail sharpening with spots; C.JPG defaults, a process 2 edit, a Tone Curve with spots; D.ARW defaults, Detail radius/masking with Color noise reduction and an angle): **9 of 9 identical** to main's hashes recorded in Part 14's final run (full-size and 1024 px export SHA-256, 512 px thumbnail, histogram statistics). The rendering code of main `19015b0` is that run's code.
- **Bundle**: the main chunk grew from 974.70 kB to 977.55 kB (+2.85 kB; gzipped 311.96 → 313.19 kB, +1.23 kB): the lazy import, the engine's instrument option and pipeline labels, and the export names the lazy chunk imports from the main chunk. The benchmark itself is in its own chunk (above).
- **Unit tests**: `bench/bench.test.ts` (median and min for odd and even counts, unsorted input, non-finite values and none at all; pass sums; rounding detection; ms and byte formatting; the commit from the version; the Markdown's header facts, table, sub-rows, escaped pipes and failed rows, and its wall-clock-only, scaled and cancelled lines; the JSON round trip; the test images' sizes, levels, patterns and determinism; the texture memory estimate) and `bench/plan.test.ts` (what Quick and Full run, scaled images, a library photo's label). `npm ci`, `npx tsc -b`, `npx oxlint` (the two existing warnings), `npx vitest run` (77 files, 748 tests; main `19015b0` has 75 and 730, so 18 new) and `npm run build` passed.

### Limitations and unverified

- **Not run on a real GPU** here. The owner's first runs (one NVIDIA laptop GPU) are under "Owner's results" below; there are no AMD, Intel or Apple numbers yet.
- **Timestamp queries** are what the browser reports: Chrome rounds them to 0.1 ms by default, and some drivers report overlapping or reordered passes oddly. The wall-clock times are the reference; the GPU times split them up.
- The test images are synthetic: speeds that depend on content (highlight reconstruction's clipped area, Remove's search) can differ on real photos. The library photo option covers that; C20a's fixed set of real photos (D700, A6300, X100F, 1D Mark III at ISO 6400) isn't bundled.
- **"Use a photo from my library" wasn't tested end to end** (the test library is empty); it reads the photo as Export does (`getPhoto`, `openPhotoFile`, the decoder).
- Peak memory estimates the engine's own allocations, not what the driver or the GPU process uses; on SwiftShader it says nothing about video memory.
- The display pass is measured on the benchmark's canvas, not Develop's; at another canvas size the fitted view's cost changes.
- Remove's fill search runs on the CPU, so its row measures the CPU as much as the GPU; the JPEG row is CPU only.
- No release gate yet (PLAN, "Every release"): the numbers are for the owner to read and paste, not checked against budgets.


### Owner's results (2026-10-14)

Two Full runs (2 warm-ups, 5 measured runs per stage) pasted by the owner from System Check › GPU benchmark, app 0.6.0 (`5307029`), Edge 155 on Windows x64. Adapter: nvidia · lovelace (the owner's RTX 4000 Ada Laptop GPU, 12 GB; not a fallback adapter), `timestamp-query` and `shader-f16` available; `maxTextureDimension2D` 16384, `maxBufferSize` 2 GiB, `maxStorageBufferBindingSize` 2147483644. Canvas mode: canvas (probe-ok), DPR 1. The test images are the synthetic ones above. The GPU times step in multiples of about 0.066 ms, so sub-0.1 ms GPU figures only say "very small".

Run 1 (wall-clock median / GPU median / peak memory):

| Stage | Image | Wall | GPU | Peak memory |
|---|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Stage A: RAW → scene | Bayer 24 MP | 75.6 ms | 14.2 ms | 1.21 GB |
