- Whether Lightroom adds a history step when a snapshot is clicked, and its exact default name format, are not known; LightBench's behaviour here is its own.
- **Ctrl+N in an installed app window** rests on Chromium's source as quoted by Cowork; it was tested here with `display-mode: standalone` emulated, not in a real installed Chrome or Edge window, and Edge wasn't checked. Whether Firefox delivers Ctrl+N to a page wasn't checked (LightBench doesn't take it in a tab anyway).
- The hover preview and Set as Before show the snapshot's settings with the photo's current RAW stage (highlight reconstruction, demosaic): a snapshot that differs only there looks the same in the preview and in Before as now (Before and preset previews already work this way). Applying it does change the RAW stage.
- Before / after is still full-frame only; a split or side-by-side comparison (darktable, Lightroom) is a later step (research Q A6).
- No limit on the number or size of snapshots (research Q A4); each holds full settings, so many snapshots of a photo with many Remove spots make a larger sidecar.
- Change detection compares the original's snapshots; a virtual copy's are read when the copy is made from the sidecar (a fresh library), as copies' other fields are, not merged later.
- A photo with snapshots but no edit whose last snapshot another LightBench deletes leaves a file without LightBench's fields, which reads as "unknown" rather than "none", so that deletion isn't picked up (the snapshot stays).

## Part 20: Soft proofing

**What it does.** Develop can show a photo as a printer and paper, or another colour space, will reproduce it: Lightroom Classic's **soft proofing**.
- **S** turns it on and off (also the **Soft proofing** checkbox in Develop's toolbar); **Shift+S** shows or hides the **destination gamut warning**.
- A bar under the toolbar, while proofing: the destination **Profile** (built in, or loaded from disk), **Load profile…** and **Remove profile**, the rendering **Intent**, **Black point compensation**, **Simulate Paper & Ink**, **Screen warning (sRGB)** or **(Display P3)**, **Destination warning** and **Create Proof Copy**; a status line ("Proofing doesn't change exports; the histogram shows …").
- A badge at the bottom right of the photo, "Proof preview: <profile> · <intent>", and the surround drawn in the proof's paper white.
- The **histogram follows the proof**: for RGB (and grey) profiles it shows the destination's own values, for CMYK the proofed colours in sRGB.

It is a way of viewing, never part of the edit: proofing changes no setting, no history step, no sidecar, no export and no thumbnail. With it off, every drawing is made exactly as before (byte-identical, below).

This part is numbered 20: Part 19 is the snapshots build.

Owner's decisions (2026-10-14, after [research/SNAPSHOTS_PROOFING_SEARCH.md](research/SNAPSHOTS_PROOFING_SEARCH.md) Part B and its Cowork check, [research/cowork-2026-10-13b/E_SNAPSHOTS_PROOFING_SEARCH.md](research/cowork-2026-10-13b/E_SNAPSHOTS_PROOFING_SEARCH.md)):
1. **Soft proofing like Lightroom's:** S and Shift+S; a proof bar with profile, intent, Simulate Paper & Ink, a screen gamut warning labelled honestly (sRGB or Display P3: browsers expose no monitor profile), a destination gamut warning, and Create Proof Copy naming the copy after the profile; the histogram follows the proof.
2. **Colour management:** lcms-wasm (npm, MIT, 1.0.5) in a worker, baking a 3D table for working space → destination (with the paper if simulated) → display; the display shader samples it only while proofing; the warning colour drawn by LightBench's shader; the table cached per profile, intent and options.
3. **Profiles:** sRGB, an Adobe RGB (1998)-compatible profile and Display P3 built in, from licence-clean sources; printer and paper ICC files loaded from disk, kept locally (OPFS), removable; LUT-based (CMYK, printer) profiles through Little CMS; RGB destinations as Lightroom, CMYK optional.
4. **Intent:** Relative Colorimetric with black point compensation by default; Perceptual, Saturation and Absolute selectable (Absolute without it).
5. **Firefox:** no 2D canvas colour space; detected, sRGB with a note.
6. **Export:** unchanged by proofing; "Export with this profile" only if trivial, otherwise later.
7. **Storage:** a view state, remembered per browser, not in sidecars.

### What Lightroom does (as far as checked)

| Fact | Source |
|---|---|
| **S** "Expand / collapse soft proofing"; **Shift+S** "Hide / unhide Gamut destination warning in Soft Proofing". | Adobe's Lightroom Classic shortcut table (updated 2025-10-29), quoted by Cowork |
| The histogram while proofing "is based on the icc profile". | Adobe Community expert, 2023 (secondary) |
| Create Proof Copy puts the proof profile in the virtual copy's Copy Name field. | Adobe Community, 2018, marked correct (secondary) |
| Lightroom Classic proofs to RGB profiles; CMYK soft proofing was in 6.0 and removed in 6.3. | Adobe Community idea thread (secondary) |
| Monitor (blue) and destination (red) gamut warnings; Perceptual and Relative intents; Simulate Paper & Ink. | Search summaries of tutorials only (research brief, "[search]") |
| **Not found:** Adobe's soft-proofing help page (blocked), the official profile list, whether proofing changes Export, the warning colours' exact use, what Simulate Paper & Ink computes. | — |

### How it works

| Step | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Where | In the display shader (`develop-display.wgsl` `fs`), after the develop transform (… sigmoid → Tone Curve) and before the output profile: the pixel's display-referred linear Rec.2020, clamped to 0…1 and encoded with the sRGB curve, is looked up in the proof table (trilinear, eight `textureLoad`s, f32) and replaced by the proofed colour. The rest of the path is unchanged: the Display P3 or sRGB encoding, the clipping indicators (J, which now judge the proofed colours), then the warnings. | Owner's decision 2; the research brief's design |
| When | Only when `Indicators.proof` is set: Develop passes it for its canvas (pass 0) and its histogram (pass 1). Export, thumbnails, Auto, the background developer, the culling and 100 % renderers, the Tone Curve's and the mixer's own passes never pass it, so they never see the proof. With proofing off the Proof uniform (binding 13) is all zero and a 1×1×1 texture is bound at 12; the shader skips the stage. | Owner's decisions 2 and 6 |
| The table | `engine/proof/lut.ts`. 65 × 65 × 65 grid points over the encoded working space, as an N × N × 2N `rgba32float` 3D texture (8.8 MB): slices 0…N−1 the proofed colour (encoded linear Rec.2020, sign kept) with the gamut test's ΔE in alpha; slices N…2N−1 the destination's device values (RGB and grey destinations). | LightBench; LightCraft also uses a table (33³) |
| Proofed colour | Little CMS, float transforms: working space → destination with the chosen intent and black point compensation; the device values clamped to the device's range (a float transform doesn't clamp at a matrix profile's gamut, so without this an sRGB proof would show nothing); destination → linear Rec.2020 with **Relative Colorimetric and black point compensation** (paper white and ink black shown as the screen's), or with **Simulate Paper & Ink**, **Absolute Colorimetric without it** (the paper's white from its media white tag, the ink's black as the tables give it). | LightBench, following Little CMS's proofing transform; with Simulate Paper & Ink on the two agree to 0.00 8-bit levels (proof.test.ts) |
| Destination gamut test | A colorimetric round trip: the colour → destination (Relative Colorimetric, black point compensation as set, clamped) → Lab, against the colour → Lab; CIE76 ΔE above **2** is outside (`GAMUT_DE`, LightBench's choice). It interpolates smoothly between grid points, unlike Little CMS's own in-or-out gamut check (`cmsFLAGS_GAMUTCHECK`, darktable's way), which it agrees with on 95.5 % of 3000 random colours for the test printer (proof.test.ts). | lcms's gamut check is also a round trip with a threshold; LightCraft's is a round-trip distance |
| Warnings | Drawn by the display shader (lcms-wasm doesn't export `cmsSetAlarmCodes`): **destination** red, **screen** blue, **both** magenta (LightBench's colours; converted for a Display P3 canvas). The screen warning tests the proofed colour against the canvas's colour space, sRGB or Display P3, with 2/255 of slack for the table's interpolation, and its button says which. | Owner's decision 1; browsers expose only `color-gamut`, no monitor profile (Media Queries 4, via Cowork) |
| Histogram | Pass 1 with the proof: the destination's device values for RGB and grey profiles (the same histogram and clipping counts as before, on those values), the proofed colour's sRGB for CMYK. | Owner's decision 1 |
| Surround | The canvas's background shows the proof's paper: white without Simulate Paper & Ink, the paper's colour with it. | The research brief (LightCraft's "paper-white surround") |
| Worker and cache | `proof.worker.ts` loads lcms-wasm (its `lcms.wasm` through Vite's `?url`) on first use; `client.ts` keeps the last three tables by key (profile hash, intent, black point compensation, Simulate Paper & Ink, size) and builds only on a change; a failed build isn't kept. The warnings aren't part of the key (the shader draws them from the same table). Develop keeps the last table on screen until the new one is ready ("Building the proof…"). | Owner's decision 2 |
| GPU robustness (Part 14) | The table texture and the uniform belong to the engine: `unload()` frees the table and zeroes the uniform, `destroy()` frees both; a new engine after a lost device uploads the table again from the copy Develop keeps, on its first proofed drawing. The upload and the drawing run inside Develop's render loop, under the foreground gate. `guarded` isn't used on Develop's engine (Part 14's rule); the benchmark's proof rows run inside its `guarded` calls. Building the table is CPU work in the worker. | Part 14 |

### Profiles

| Profile | Source |
|---|---|
| **sRGB** | IEC 61966-2-1 primaries, D65 and the sRGB curve (`icc.ts` `SRGB_PROFILE`). |
| **Adobe RGB (1998)-compatible** | The primaries, D65 and gamma 563/256 published in Adobe's "Adobe RGB (1998) Color Image Encoding" specification (`icc.ts` `ADOBE_RGB_PROFILE`). Named "compatible" and described "LightBench" so it isn't taken for Adobe's file. |
| **Display P3** | DCI-P3 primaries, D65 and the sRGB curve (`icc.ts` `DISPLAY_P3_PROFILE`). |
| Working space | Rec.2020 (ITU-R BT.2020 primaries, D65) with the sRGB curve (the table's input) and linear (its output). |

All are written at run time by LightBench's own ICC writer (`icc.ts` `buildIcc`, which Export already uses to embed its profiles): ICC v2.1 matrix/TRC display profiles, colorants D50-adapted with Bradford, parametric curves as 1024-entry tables, copyright "No copyright, use freely". No profile file of anyone else's is shipped. lcms-wasm doesn't export `cmsCreateRGBProfile` or `cmsSaveProfileToMem`, so they couldn't be made with it; instead the built-in sRGB is checked against Little CMS's own `cmsCreate_sRGBProfile` (within 2·10⁻³) and all three against LightBench's matrices (within 2·10⁻³) in `proof.test.ts`.

**Loaded profiles** (`engine/proof/store.ts`): **Load profile…** reads an `.icc`/`.icm` file from disk (never uploaded). The worker checks it (an ICC header; not a device link, abstract or named-colour profile; a colour space of RGB, CMYK, CMY or grey; Little CMS can make transforms to and from it) and reads its description. It is kept in the browser's private storage (OPFS, folder `proof-profiles`, one file per profile named by the first 16 hex digits of its SHA-256, plus `index.json`), listed under "Loaded in this browser" with its colour space, and selected. Loading the same file again keeps one copy. **Remove profile** (asks first) deletes the file and its entry; a removed profile falls back to sRGB. Over 32 MB is refused.

**CMYK works**: Little CMS proofs to CMYK (and CMY) printer profiles as to RGB ones, through their tables (tested with a CMYK test printer, below). Their device values aren't shown in the histogram (it shows the proofed colours instead).

### Settings (`engine/proof/settings.ts`)

| Setting | Default | Note |
|---|---|---|
| On | off | S, or the toolbar checkbox |
| Profile | sRGB | `builtin:<id>` or `user:<hash>` |
| Intent | Relative Colorimetric | Perceptual, Saturation and Absolute Colorimetric too. Matrix profiles (sRGB, Adobe RGB-compatible, Display P3) have no perceptual or saturation tables, so all intents but Absolute look alike for them (Little CMS's fallback). |
| Black point compensation | on | Disabled (and not used) with Absolute Colorimetric |
| Simulate Paper & Ink | off | |
| Screen warning, Destination warning | off | Shift+S toggles the destination warning, turning proofing on if it was off |

Kept in this browser under `lightbench.proof` (localStorage), read back defensively (`cleanProof`). Not in the catalog's edits, the history, presets, Copy/Paste or sidecars.

### Create Proof Copy

Saves a change still waiting, makes a virtual copy of the photo (Phase 2, Part 6) named **"Proof: <profile name>"** (a second one gets " (2)", as copies do), copies the thumbnail, schedules its sidecar entry, and opens the copy in Develop next to the original in the filmstrip, so the photo can be adjusted for that output while the original stays as it is. Lightroom puts the profile's name in the copy's Copy Name field (secondary source); the "Proof: " prefix is LightBench's.

*Fixed 2026-10-17:* opening the copy (or any other photo after a save) could log one `ERR_FILE_NOT_FOUND`: Develop's placeholder (the thumbnail shown while a photo loads) kept a URL from the Library's thumbnail cache, which revokes it 1 s after a save replaces that thumbnail, and the next photo's loading view still showed the old photo's URL until its own was read. The placeholder now reads its own object URL (`holdThumbUrl` in `catalog/thumbs.ts`), is shown only for the photo (and load) it was read for, and is revoked in the effect's cleanup, after the render that stopped showing it, so no revoked URL is rendered and none is leaked (unit test `thumbs.test.ts`; e2e: edits, Create Proof Copy and moving between four photos with no failed `blob:` request; before the fix the same run logged one).

### Firefox and the screen's colour space

Develop draws in Display P3 on wide-gamut screens (Part 4). MDN's compatibility data lists no 2D canvas `colorSpace` option in Firefox (Cowork), so Display P3 values would be shown as sRGB there. `display-space.ts` now asks the browser itself, `canvasP3Supported()`: a 2D context made with `colorSpace: 'display-p3'` reports it (`getContextAttributes().colorSpace`) or its pixels do (`ImageData.colorSpace`). Where neither does, `currentDisplaySpace()` is sRGB everywhere it is used (Develop, the Library's loupe, culling and Survey canvases, the System Check renderer), and the screen warning's tooltip says why ("…this browser's canvases can't hold it…, so LightBench shows sRGB"). Chrome, Edge and Safari (which report it) are unchanged. Not tried in Firefox here (no Firefox in this environment).

### Export

Proofing never changes Export (nor thumbnails). Export already writes sRGB, Display P3 and Adobe RGB (Part 4's choices), so a built-in proof can be matched there by hand. Converting an export to a **loaded printer profile** would need Little CMS in the export pipeline (and the export's encoders to embed that profile): not trivial, so **left for later** (owner's decision 6), and said in the help.

### Shortcuts added

| Action | Where | Keys (both sets) |
|---|---|---|
| Soft proofing on or off | Develop | S |
| Soft proofing: destination gamut warning | Develop | Shift+S |

S and Shift+S were free in Develop; the Library's S and Shift+S (stacks) are in another view, Ctrl+Shift+S (Sync) and Ctrl+Alt+Shift+S (the mixer's Saturation target) are other chords, and the on-photo tools' contexts (crop, Guided Upright, the curve and mixer targets, the Remove brush) don't have them. `keymap.test.ts` checks this and both sets stay free of conflicts.

### Verified

Headless Chromium 141 with software WebGPU (SwiftShader), the app loaded with `?gpu=readback`, one test at a time. Scripts in `scratchpad/proof/e2e/` (Part 17's `identity.mjs`, Part 16's benchmark script and Part 15's recovery set-up copied and adapted; the originals unchanged). Test files: Nikon D700 `philadelphia.nef`, Nikon D80 `glarus.nef`, Sony ILCE-6300 ARW, `test-srgb.jpg`, and `patches.jpg` (twelve 220 × 193 patches: sRGB primaries and secondaries, skin, dark and light grey, dark green, mid blue, orange; made for this part). The printer profiles are **generated with Little CMS** (the system's liblcms2 2.14 through Python ctypes, `make_printer_icc.py`): a v4 output-class profile, RGB or CMYK, with A2B/B2A CLUTs for a printer-like gamut (smaller than sRGB's in greens and blues, gamma 1.8, ink black about L* 12.5, a paper white darker and warmer than D50). Not a real device.

- **Proofing off is byte-identical to main** (`5307029`, 0.6.0; `identity.mjs`, main served from its own copy of the tree, each run alone). Fourteen edits rendered on main and on this branch, six SHA-256 hashes each (full-size 8-bit export, 1024 px export, 512 px thumbnail, histogram statistics, Develop canvas at fit and at 100 %): **84 of 84 identical**. The edits are Part 17's: D700 process 1 with crop, turn and angle; process 2 with angle; D700 defaults (sharpened); D80 custom white balance; the JPEG unedited, process 2 and process 1; ILCE-6300 with angle; D700 with Remove/Heal/Clone spots, crop, angle and custom white balance; D700 with spots, a tone curve and sharpening; the JPEG with spots, a tone curve and Detail; the JPEG with a `colourMixer` at its defaults; the JPEG with a Colour Mixer; the D700 with a mixer, Point Color, spots, a tone curve and Detail. Part 17's fifteenth edit (the D80 with a `colourGrading` at its defaults) failed in main's run with a storage error ("A requested file or directory could not be found", reading the file from the page's private storage; not investigated); on this branch its six hashes equal the ones Part 17 recorded for it on main `7b8b0f0` (`f34d827d0e1ed7bc…`). In Develop, turning proofing on and off again gave the canvas back to the level (below).
- **GPU = CPU** (`gpucpu-proof.mjs`). Develop's canvas drawn with the proof table at 100 % (read back, 8-bit sRGB) against Little CMS run **per pixel** in the page (`ProofChain`, lcms-wasm) on the same developed pixels (the export pass's encoded Rec.2020, 16-bit), converted to 8-bit sRGB the same way; 65³ table. Three images (`patches.jpg` with Saturation +40, 960 × 640; `test-srgb.jpg` with Vibrance +20, 1600 × 1072; the D700 cropped to 1070 × 710, +0.3 EV) × sRGB, Display P3, the test printer RGB and CMYK × Relative, Perceptual and Simulate Paper & Ink (Display P3: Relative only): **mean difference 0.005–0.14 levels, at most 7 levels in one channel, under 0.9 % of channels over 1 level; mean ΔE76 0.006–0.20, max ΔE76 2.12** (the test printer CMYK, Perceptual, on the D700). The baseline without the table (the canvas against the same CPU conversion of the 16-bit export pixels) already differs by up to 1.54 ΔE76 (16-bit float in the export pass against 32-bit in the canvas), so most of the maximum isn't the table's. Stated tolerance: mean ≤ 0.5 levels and max ΔE76 ≤ 3, met by all 33 cases.

  | Case (D700 crop) | mean |Δ| (levels) | max |Δ| | channels > 1 level | mean ΔE76 | max ΔE76 |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | proofing off (baseline) | 0.011 | 1 | 0 % | 0.019 | 1.54 |
  | sRGB, Relative | 0.012 | 4 | 0.001 % | 0.021 | 1.51 |
  | Display P3, Relative | 0.012 | 1 | 0 % | 0.021 | 1.57 |
  | test printer RGB, Relative | 0.041 | 5 | 0.001 % | 0.064 | 1.55 |
  | test printer RGB, Perceptual | 0.11 | 5 | 0.82 % | 0.147 | 1.82 |
  | test printer RGB, Simulate Paper & Ink | 0.031 | 2 | 0 % | 0.049 | 1.48 |
  | test printer CMYK, Relative | 0.052 | 5 | 0.01 % | 0.087 | 1.68 |
  | test printer CMYK, Perceptual | 0.138 | 5 | 0.62 % | 0.201 | 2.11 |
- **In Develop** (`e2e-proof.mjs`, `patches.jpg`; **29 of 29** on the final code; earlier runs failed only on the script's own waits and on comparing in 8-bit levels, see the sRGB item):
  - **S** showed the bar and "Proof preview: sRGB · Relative Colorimetric", Relative with black point compensation on and both warnings off. The sRGB photo proofed to sRGB: every patch within **0.87 ΔE76** of the unproofed canvas (in 8-bit values, a near-zero channel of the pure primaries moved by up to 17 levels: the gamut edge's clamp inside grid cells, see Limitations). The surround was white (255, 255, 255).
  - **Loading** the Little CMS-generated printer profile (the file input) selected it under "Loaded in this browser": saturated blue went from (0, 0, 254) to (9, 86, 236), the dark grey stayed (60, 60, 60); the histogram changed; the status said "Proofing doesn't change exports; the histogram shows LB Test Lustre (generated)'s own values".
  - **Shift+S** marked red, green, blue, cyan, magenta, yellow, mid blue and orange in red, not the greys or skin; Shift+S again removed it exactly.
  - **Perceptual** moved saturated blue (9, 86, 236 → 0, 86, 236). **Simulate Paper & Ink**: light grey 230 → (216, 221, 207), dark grey 60 → (64, 66, 61), surround (238, 244, 228).
  - **Screen warning (sRGB)** with the Adobe RGB (1998)-compatible proof and Saturation +100: ten patches blue (all but the greys); with the destination warning too, magenta.
  - **Create Proof Copy** made a virtual copy "Proof: LB Test Lustre (generated)" (version 1 of P.JPG in the catalog) and Develop showed it, selected, in the filmstrip.
  - **Reload:** proofing on, the loaded profile and Relative remembered; blue drawn exactly as before (9, 86, 236). `lightbench.proof` in localStorage held the settings; no edit in the catalog mentions the proof.
  - **Recovery:** with the destination warning on, Develop's device was destroyed; a new engine drew the proof and the warning again (the blue patch red, the greys unchanged), no error.
  - **S** again: the canvas equalled the canvas before proofing to the level (max difference 0).
  - **Remove profile** (confirmed) fell back to sRGB, emptied the list and deleted the `.icc` file from the browser's storage (only `index.json` left).
  - Screenshots checked by eye: the bar, the destination warning, Simulate Paper & Ink with its paper-coloured surround, the screen warning, the recovered view.
- **Benchmark** (Part 16's Quick e2e with the proof rows, `e2e-bench-proof.mjs`; `?benchmp=0.3`): **41 of 41**: 22 stage rows, the two soft-proofing rows after the display rows and before Remove, none failed. SwiftShader at 0.3 MP (**says nothing about a real GPU**): "Soft proofing on: display pass, fit (both gamut warnings)" 1557 ms median (GPU 1550 ms, peak 25 MB), against "Display pass, fit, without Tone Curve" 2507 ms in the same run (SwiftShader's noise is larger than the difference); "Soft proof table: build 65³ with Little CMS (worker)" **327 ms** median (318 ms min) for the Adobe RGB-compatible profile, in the worker. After each image's `unload()` 37,016 bytes stayed allocated (Part 17: 36,936): the 64-byte Proof uniform and the 16-byte empty table, which belong to the engine; the 8.8 MB table was freed.
- **Table build and accuracy** (`proof.test.ts`, Node 22): 33³ in 53–84 ms, 65³ in 0.31–0.54 s, per profile and options (the sRGB profile and the test printer). Against Little CMS per pixel on 4000 random colours, in encoded units: 33³ mean 0.15–0.19, max 1.7–2.1 8-bit levels; 65³ mean 0.04–0.06, max 0.95–1.06.
- **Unit tests** (`npx vitest run`: **902 tests in 94 files**; main `5307029`: 872 in 91, so 30 new):
  - `engine/proof/proof.test.ts` (21): the built-in profiles are ICC display profiles Little CMS reads, named as LightBench's; built-in sRGB transforms like Little CMS's own sRGB within 2·10⁻³; the three built-ins agree with `icc.ts`'s matrices within 2·10⁻³; **identity** (the working space as destination: the table within 2·10⁻³ of the identity, nothing out of gamut, paper white 1); **sRGB → sRGB** (sRGB colours back within 0.84 levels, in gamut); **gamut test** on known colours (Rec.2020 primaries out of sRGB, saturated sRGB blue, green and cyan out of the test printer, greys and skin in; Display P3 holds sRGB green but not Rec.2020's); agreement with Little CMS's gamut check (95.5 %) and with its proofing transform with Simulate Paper & Ink (0.00 levels); clipping at an RGB destination's gamut; **intents** (Perceptual differs on saturated colours, not on greys; Saturation and Absolute build); **black point compensation** (shadow detail kept with it, dark greys collapsed into the ink black without); Simulate Paper & Ink (the paper darker and warmer, the ink's black lifted; off: white and black as the screen's); **CMYK** destinations (no device values; close to the same printer driven as RGB); RGB destinations' device values; refusing non-profiles and device links; the 33³/65³ accuracy above; settings defaults and cleaning; Absolute disables black point compensation; **cache keys** differ exactly when the tables would (profile, intent, BPC, paper, size; not the warnings); profile hashes; the grid order.
  - `engine/proof/store.test.ts` (3): **load and remove** (kept, listed, read back, one copy for the same file, removed with its file, listeners told); refused files keep nothing; built-in profiles resolve without storage.
  - `engine/gpu/proof-gpu.test.ts` (3): the table uploaded as an N × N × 2N 3D texture once per key, the old one retired; `unload` frees it and zeroes the uniform once; the WGSL Proof struct (64 bytes) at bindings 12 and 13, skipped when off.
  - `settings/keymap.test.ts` (+1: S and Shift+S in Develop only, the Library's stack keys and Ctrl+Shift+S untouched, not in the on-photo tools' contexts), `engine/gpu/display-space.test.ts` (+1: sRGB and a note where canvases can't hold Display P3), `bench/plan.test.ts` (+1: the proof rows and their place).
- From a clean `npm ci`: `npx tsc -b`, `npx oxlint` (the two existing warnings), `npx vitest run` and `npm run build` passed. `npm ci` now also prints a deprecation warning for `glob@7.2.3` (a dependency of `tape`, which lcms-wasm lists; not shipped), and the build notes lcms-wasm's Node branch (`import("module")`, `new URL("./", import.meta.url)`), which the browser never runs.
- **Bundle:** the main chunk grew from 1,116.63 kB (356.25 kB gzipped) to 1,136.19 kB (362.85 kB gzipped): +19.6 kB (+6.6 kB gzipped) for the proof bar, its state, the profile store, the worker client, the shader and engine changes and the built-in profiles' definitions. Little CMS stays out of it: `proof.worker-*.js` 44.19 kB and `lcms-*.wasm` 315.91 kB (118.03 kB gzipped) load only when soft proofing is first used. The service worker precaches them with the rest of the app for offline use (30 files, was 27). `dist` 6,983,488 → 7,365,071 bytes.

### Limitations and unverified

- **Lightroom:** nothing was compared with Lightroom. Its proof maths, warning colours, Simulate Paper & Ink, profile list and Export behaviour aren't public or weren't reachable (Adobe's help page was blocked for Cowork). The "Proof: " prefix and the surround are LightBench's.
- **Real printer profiles:** none was available; the tests use printer-like profiles generated with Little CMS (and LightBench's own test writer). How long a real printer profile's table takes to build was not measured.
- **Monitor:** the screen warning tests sRGB or Display P3, not the monitor; the browser maps the canvas to the monitor itself. Not checked on a calibrated wide-gamut monitor or in Firefox (the fallback is unit-tested with a stand-in document).
- **Interpolation:** the 65³ table differs from Little CMS per pixel by at most about one 8-bit level in the working space's encoding; in sRGB values, a channel near 0 of a colour on the sRGB boundary can move more (17 levels, 0.87 ΔE76, on pure sRGB primaries proofed to sRGB), because the clamp at the gamut's edge falls inside grid cells.
- **Gamut test:** the 2 ΔE threshold is LightBench's. It agrees with Little CMS's own gamut check on 95.5 % of random colours for the test printer; the colours where they differ weren't examined.
- **Speed:** the table build was timed in Node and on SwiftShader only; the display pass's extra cost on a real GPU isn't known.
- Not built: offering a proof copy when an edit starts while proofing (Lightroom does, per search summaries only); before/after against the unproofed photo (Before is proofed too); a monitor profile (impossible in browsers); "Export with this profile" for loaded profiles.


## Part 21: Section eyes and Tone Curve Auto

**What it does.** Two things in Develop's tool stack.
1. **Section eyes.** Besides each group's eye (Part 1), every section inside the groups has its own eye: Crop, Transform and Lens in Crop & Lens; White balance, Tone and Presence in Basic; Regions, RGB, Red, Green and Blue in the Tone Curve; Mixer and Point Color in the Colour Mixer; each wheel (Shadows, Midtones, Highlights, Global) in Colour Grading; Sharpening and Noise reduction in Detail. Remove keeps its single eye. A section's eye shows the photo, and the histogram, with just that section at its default. It is a view only, like the group eyes: nothing is saved, the history doesn't change, Export is unaffected.
2. **Tone Curve Auto.** An **Auto** row under the curve's presets: **Contrast** (a gentle S-curve fitted to this photo), **Colour** (small Red, Green and Blue curve moves that neutralise a colour cast), **Suggestions** (the curve presets and both Auto results drawn on this photo) and an **Amount** for the result just applied. Each result is one undoable history step.

Owner's decisions (2026-10-14): "not all modules have eyes... the white balance and tone sections within don't have eyes"; for Tone Curve Auto, all three options (Auto contrast, Auto colour, suggestions), and "Keep as is" for the group's layout, so the Auto row is added under the presets and nothing else moves.

### Section eyes (`develop/sectionEyes.ts`, `SectionEye.tsx`, `sectionEyeContext.ts`)

| Group | Section | Hidden means (its own Reset, or as noted) | Its eye shows when |
|---|---|---|---|
| Crop & Lens | Crop | Crop, straighten angle, rotation and flips at their defaults; Transform/Upright kept | the photo is cropped, straightened, turned or flipped |
| | Transform | No Upright and Transform sliders (the Transform section's Reset); the crop kept | the transform changes the photo |
| | Lens | Lens corrections off (there is no Lens Reset; this is what the group eye already did) | corrections are applied to this photo |
| Basic | White balance | **As Shot** (the photo kind's default), with the RAW stage rebuilt (below) | the white balance isn't As Shot |
| | Tone | Exposure, Contrast, Highlights, Shadows, Whites and Blacks at the photo kind's defaults (0) | any of them differs |
| | Presence | Vibrance and Saturation at 0 | either differs |
| Tone Curve | Regions | Region sliders 0, splits 25/50/75 | a region slider isn't 0 |
| | RGB, Red, Green, Blue | That curve a straight line (Reset RGB / Red / …) | that curve isn't straight |
| Colour Mixer | Mixer | The 24 sliders at 0, Point Color and Advanced kept (the mixer's Reset) | a slider isn't 0 |
| | Point Color | No picked colours (Remove all) | a colour is picked |
| Colour Grading | Shadows, Midtones, Highlights, Global | That wheel at its centre with Luminance 0 (Reset Shadows / …); Blending, Balance and Advanced kept | the wheel has a saturation or luminance (a hue alone changes nothing, as the view's dot says) |
| Detail | Sharpening | Sharpening as its Reset sets it: **on for RAW photos** (Amount 40, or 25 above ISO 1000, Radius 1.0, Detail 25, Masking 0), off for JPEGs | sharpening differs from the photo's default |
| | Noise reduction | Off, with Lightroom's default sub-sliders (its Reset) | it differs |

| Behaviour | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Where the eyes are | In the section's heading, right-aligned like the group eye (the Crop eye at the end of the Crop… / Reset row, which has no heading). The Tone Curve, Colour Mixer and Colour Grading show their sections as view tabs, so not all are on screen at once: their eyes are a row of small labelled eyes under the tabs ("RGB", "R", "Point Color", "Shadows"…), listing every section that is changed or hidden. Same eye icon, size and colours as the group eye (`.tool-group-eye`). | Owner's request; the placement is LightBench's |
| When an eye shows | Once its section differs from its default, as the group eyes appear once their group changes something. An eye that is off **always stays**, even if its section is reset meanwhile; it goes once turned on again. An unedited photo shows none. | Task requirement |
| Tooltips | "Show the photo without its white balance, As Shot instead (nothing is changed)", "Show the photo without the Red curve (nothing is changed)", …, and "Show White balance again" when off: the group eyes' style. | Part 1 |
| Badge | "Hidden: …" lists hidden sections in the stack's order, e.g. "Hidden: White balance, Red curve". A hidden group is named as before ("Tone Curve"), not its sections. | Task requirement |
| Group and section eyes | Hidden sections are set to their defaults first, then hidden groups (`applyEyes`), so **the group eye overrides**: with the Tone Curve's eye off the whole curve is hidden whatever its sections' eyes say. The sections' states are **kept**: their eyes stay, marked off and dimmed, with a tooltip saying the group hides them; when the group is shown again, the sections that are off stay hidden. Eyes of other groups' sections still apply. With no section eye off, the group eyes give exactly what they gave before (unit test). | LightBench's definition |
| Another photo | Every eye, group and section, is on again when another photo is shown. **Note:** on `main` the group eyes were *not* reset when moving to another photo inside Develop (the filmstrip; found by reading the code: the state lived on in `DevelopView`, which isn't remounted); they are now, with the section eyes. | Task requirement |
| While cropping | The crop being edited is shown; Crop & Lens's eyes (group and sections) don't apply, as the group eye didn't. | Part 1 |
| With Snapshots and soft proofing (Parts 19, 20) | Before (\\), including a snapshot set as Before, shows that state as it is, without any eye, as before. A hovered snapshot or preset is shown with the eyes that are off applied, as the group eyes always did. Soft proofing works on what is drawn, eyes included. | Parts 1, 19, 20 |
| Exactness | Each section's hidden form is computed by the same functions its Reset uses (`withToneCurve`, `withColourMixer`/`withPoints`, `withColourGrading`, `withDetail`, the photo kind's `defaultsFor`), so a hidden section renders exactly the photo with that section reset (unit tests on the settings and the GPU uniforms; e2e on the pixels, below). | Task requirement |
| White balance and the RAW stage | A RAW's white balance is applied before highlight reconstruction and demosaic (Stage A); the display pass only corrects a different white balance by a ratio, which isn't exactly the photo developed with it. So while white balance is hidden (its eye, or **Basic's group eye**, which hides it too) Stage A is rebuilt with As Shot (300 ms after the toggle, as after a white balance change), and the view is pixel-identical to the reset photo. Work that must see the photo's real settings meanwhile — its thumbnail after a save, Basic's Auto, the Tone Curve's Auto and suggestions — builds the real stage first and the view's again afterwards (`onRealStage`). JPEGs have no such stage. **This changes Part 1's Basic eye**, which was the display-ratio approximation and is now exact (Advanced still stays, as before). | LightBench |
| Colour Grading per wheel | Maps cleanly: each wheel is its own entry in the settings (`shadows`, `midtones`, `highlights`, `global`) with its own Reset. Blending, Balance and Advanced have no eye of their own; the group eye hides them. | Task requirement |
| Detail on RAW photos | Sharpening's default is on for RAW photos, so its eye compares with that default and hiding shows the default sharpening, not none. An edit saved before Detail existed (sharpening off) therefore shows a Sharpening eye whose hidden view is sharpened, as the section's Reset would be. The group eye still shows the photo without any sharpening or noise reduction. | Task requirement ("the same defaults the Reset buttons use") |
| Crop and Transform | The Crop eye keeps Transform/Upright. **Changed afterwards (owner, 2026-10-17):** the Crop section's **Reset** now keeps them too — it resets only crop, straighten, rotate and flip (one history step “Reset crop”, as before; nothing to reset adds none), with the same neutral as the eye (`resetCrop` in `geometry.ts`). Transform's own Reset still removes Upright and the sliders; the Crop & Lens group eye still shows the whole photo. Rendering of saved edits is unchanged. | LightBench; owner |
| Shortcuts | None added. | Task requirement |

### Tone Curve Auto (`engine/develop/curve-auto.ts`, `develop/useCurveAuto.ts`, `CurveAuto.tsx`, `curveAutoState.ts`)

Both methods are **LightBench's own**, written for this tool. Photoshop has commands called Auto Contrast and Auto Color (general knowledge); Adobe's algorithms aren't published and nothing here is modelled on them; whether Lightroom's tone curve offers an Auto wasn't checked. Their input is the histogram the curve's graph already shows: the GPU stats pass of the curve's input on the curve's own axis (sRGB-encoded Rec.2020, 256 bins, `DevelopEngine.renderCurveInput`), measured on the photo's real settings, after Basic, the mixer and the grade. Both are pure functions of those bins.

| Behaviour | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Auto contrast | (1) The median tone m is the pivot and stays where it is; the 0.5th and 99.5th percentiles lo and hi (at least 2 % from black and white) are the protected ends. (2) The steepness at m is g = 0.32 / (standard deviation of the encoded luma), between 1 and 1.25: a photo already spread that widely gets a straight line, a flat one at most 1.25 (darktable's "contrast \| high" preset is about 1.18 at the middle). (3) Seven points: (0, 0), (lo, lo), a point halfway between lo and m pulled down, (m, m), a point halfway between m and hi pushed up, (hi, hi), (1, 1); the pulls make the spline's slope at m equal g. On darktable's monotone spline (Part 12) the curve then stays on or above the diagonal below lo and on or below it above hi (the end segments' Hermite form; unit-tested), so the darkest and brightest tones never move towards black or white. (4) It is checked against the curve it replaces on the same histogram, through Regions (`clipCounts`: a bin counts as black when its darkest value comes out below half a level, as white when its brightest comes out at 254.5 levels or above). If the current curve clipped fewer pixels (e.g. Fade lifts the blacks), the S-curve is blended towards the current one by bisection until it clips no more. | LightBench |
| Why the RGB point curve | Its points are visible and editable after Auto and keep their exact positions (Regions' shape is LightBench's own, with fixed limits per region), and the RGB curve is what the presets set. Regions and the channel curves are left as they were. | LightBench |
| Auto colour | Each channel's histogram of the channel curves' input (after the RGB curve and Regions) gives its 2nd, 50th and 98th percentile. At each anchor the three channels' mean is the neutral target, and each channel's curve moves its anchor towards it: fully in the midtones (neutral midtones), half-way in the shadows and highlights (near-neutral ends), by at most 4 % of the axis in the midtones and 2.5 % at the ends, and not at all for casts under 0.2 % or where an anchor is within 3 % (midtones 5 %) of black or white. Each channel curve runs through (0, 0), its moved anchors and (1, 1). White balance isn't touched; the RGB curve and Regions are kept. | LightBench |
| Auto colour's limit | Percentiles summarise whole channels, not the photo's grey pixels: a photo dominated by one colour (a blue sky, green leaves) reads as a cast and is nudged towards neutral too, within the 4 % limit. A version based on near-neutral pixels needs per-pixel data (later). | LightBench |
| Suggestions | **Suggestions ▸** draws Linear, Medium Contrast, Strong Contrast and Fade (on the RGB curve, the rest of the curve kept) and the two Auto results on this photo, 120 px, with `renderImage` (the thumbnails' path), one after another, and again 0.4 s after the photo's settings change while they are shown. Hovering one previews it on the photo (through the curve's drag preview, as hovering a preset previews it; no history step); clicking applies it. | Task requirement; preset previews (Phase 2) |
| History | Each result is **one history step of its own**: "Tone Curve: Auto contrast", "Tone Curve: Auto colour", or e.g. "Tone Curve: Fade" for a suggestion. A result equal to the current curve adds nothing and says so. Ctrl+Z / Ctrl+Y as for any step. | Task requirement |
| Amount | 0–100 %, default 100 %, shown while the result's step is the step shown: it moves the curve between the one before the result (0 %) and the result (100 %), with the presets' curve blending (`blendToneCurve`: each point curve sampled at both curves' points). Like a preset's Amount it **changes that step in place** (`replaceCurrent`), so dragging it adds no steps; undo and redo keep the Amount it had. Double-click: 100 %. | Task requirement; preset Amount |
| Determinism | The same photo and settings give the same histogram (the stats pass is deterministic) and the same curve (pure functions, nothing random). Auto contrast reads the curve's input before the RGB curve and Auto colour before the channel curves, which they replace, so running one again gives the same curve. | Task requirement |
| Process version | **None needed.** Auto only writes tone-curve settings, which the existing curve stage renders; no rendering code changed, and old edits render byte-identically (below). | Part 1's rule |
| Help | The buttons', the Amount's and Suggestions' tooltips (`help.ts`, `CURVE_AUTO_HELP`) say what each does, that white balance isn't touched, and the limits. A line under the row says what the last Auto did ("S-curve around 46.1 %, slope 1.25 there; …", "Midtones: Red −1.2 %, Green +0.6 %, Blue +0.6 % …", "This photo's tones are already spread widely: …"). | Owner's rule (guidance) |
| Shortcuts | None added. | Task requirement |

### Files

New: `develop/sectionEyes.ts` (the sections, their defaults, `applyEyes`, the badge), `develop/SectionEye.tsx` and `sectionEyeContext.ts` (the eyes, given their state by DevelopView), `develop/section-eyes.css` (imported by the components, so `index.css` is untouched), `engine/develop/curve-auto.ts` (both Auto methods, the clipping check, the Amount), `develop/curveAutoState.ts` (history), `develop/useCurveAuto.ts` and `CurveAuto.tsx` (the Auto row). Changed: the group panels (one eye or eye row each), `ToneCurvePanel.tsx` (an `auto` slot under the presets), `ToolStack.tsx` (the eye icon moved to `tools.tsx`), `DevelopView.tsx` (the eyes' state and badge, `applyEyes` in `shownOf`, the RAW stage for hidden white balance, `onRealStage`, the Auto hook), `help.ts`. The engine, the shaders and the shortcut registry are unchanged.

### Verified

Headless Chromium with software WebGPU (SwiftShader), `?gpu=readback`, on a machine shared with other test runs. Files: `test-srgb.jpg` (1600 × 1072) as C.JPG and D.JPG, and the Nikon D700 `philadelphia.nef` as A.NEF. Scratch scripts in `scratchpad/se/`: `e2e-eyes.mjs`, `e2e-auto.mjs`, `identity.mjs` / `identity-last.mjs` (Part 17's, copied).

- **Each section eye = its Reset, pixel for pixel** (`e2e-eyes.mjs`). An edit with every section changed was saved to the catalog, opened, and for each section: its eye shown; eye off → the badge "Hidden: <section>" and a changed view; eye on → the edit again, no badge, no history step; then the section's own Reset in the panel (White balance → As shot; Home on each Tone and Presence slider; Reset RGB; Reset Red; Point Color's Remove all; Sharpening's and Noise reduction's Reset), and the view (the canvas, read back, SHA-256) **and** the histogram (its canvas) compared with the hidden ones; then no eye once the section is at its default.
  - **JPEG, 8 sections** (White balance, Tone, Presence, RGB curve, Red curve, Point Color, Sharpening, Noise reduction): **all identical**, view and histogram. With the rest of the script, **78 of 78** checks.
  - **RAW (D700), 4 sections:** Tone, Sharpening (whose default is on: Amount 80 → 40) and Noise reduction identical, view and histogram (24 of 24). **White balance** identical too (8 of 8), once the script waited for the RAW stage: hiding it first draws the display-ratio approximation (`68252a…`), then the stage built with As Shot (`51212b…`, the same hash as after As shot in the panel); showing it again returns to the edit (`bad2b2…`). On SwiftShader that rebuild took 20–26 s and the next drawing 66–85 s at fit; nothing was measured on a real GPU. An earlier run that didn't wait for the stage failed these 3 checks (it compared the approximation), which is why the stage is rebuilt.
- **After merging `main` (Snapshots, soft proofing)** the JPEG script and the Auto script were run again on the merged code: Auto **29 of 29**; eyes 77 of 78, the one failure Noise reduction's histogram read while it was still being measured (with noise reduction the histogram takes seconds longer than the view on SwiftShader; the same section run alone with a 15 s histogram window: **8 of 8**, both hashes identical). The RAW part wasn't run again (the merge changed nothing on its path).
- **Which eyes show:** on the edit, eyes for exactly the changed sections (White balance, Tone, Presence, Regions, RGB, Red, Blue, Mixer, Point Color, Shadows, Highlights, Sharpening, Noise reduction), none for the unchanged ones (Green, Midtones, Global, Crop, Transform, Lens); an unedited photo had none. A hidden section reset meanwhile kept its eye (off), which went once it was turned on.
- **Badge and groups:** "Hidden: White balance, Red curve"; with the Tone Curve's group eye off and the Red eye off, "Hidden: Tone Curve" and the view equal to the group-only view, the Red eye kept, pressed and dimmed; group eye on again → "Hidden: Red curve" and the Red-hidden view again.
- **Another photo:** after turning off the Tone eye and Detail's group eye, moving to D.JPG in the filmstrip showed no eye off and no badge; back on C.JPG, all eyes on and the view as before. The catalog still held the edit's settings unchanged (nothing saved).
- **Tone Curve Auto** (`e2e-auto.mjs`, C.JPG with Contrast −45, Whites −20, Blacks +20 and a custom white balance; **29 of 29**):
  - The Auto row sits right under the presets (Contrast, Colour, Suggestions); no Amount before a result.
  - **Auto contrast**: one step "Tone Curve: Auto contrast"; 7 editable points on the RGB curve; the curve at 25 % went to 20.7 % and at 75 % to 79.8 % (an S); the readout "S-curve around 51.9 %, slope 1.25 there; tones below 10.3 % and above 92.2 % kept clear of black and white"; only `toneCurve` differed in the saved settings. **Clipping**, measured with the engine's GPU stats pass at 512 px (any RGB channel at the display limit, or all at black): 0 and 0 before and after (this photo had none to start with).
  - Ctrl+Z went back to the photo before it (Amount hidden), Ctrl+Y brought it back with its Amount. Undo and Auto again gave **the same curve** (saved JSON and view hash identical).
  - **Amount**: 50 presses of ← on the slider took it to 50 % with the history still "Tone Curve: Auto contrast | Import"; the curve at 25 % was 22.87 %, exactly half-way; undo/redo kept 50 %; double-click gave 100 % (the full result's hash).
  - **Auto colour**: one step "Tone Curve: Auto colour"; |mean R − B| of the view from 8.27 to 2.41 (this photo's sky makes it blue on average, so it was pulled towards neutral, within the limit); white balance and the RGB curve unchanged; no channel curve more than 3.75 % from the diagonal; undo and redo.
  - **Suggestions**: Linear, Medium Contrast, Strong Contrast, Fade, Auto contrast, Auto colour, each a 120 px drawing of this photo (Fade brighter than Linear: lifted blacks); hovering Strong Contrast changed the photo without a step, leaving it showed the photo again; clicking Fade added "Tone Curve: Fade" with an Amount; clicking the Auto contrast suggestion added "Tone Curve: Auto contrast".
  - Screenshots of the Tone Curve group with the suggestions and of Basic's White balance heading with its eye were checked by eye.
- **Old edits unchanged** (`identity.mjs`, Part 17's fifteen edits, each server run alone; `main` had moved on to `3044fc2`, with Snapshots and soft proofing, when it was served, and was compared with this branch before main was merged into it; the merge adds no rendering code of this part's): full-size 8-bit export, 1024 px export, 512 px thumbnail, histogram statistics, and the Develop canvas at fit and at 100 %, hashed: **all 90 hashes identical**. On `main` the last edit (the D80 with a `colourGrading` at its defaults) hit the test page's known flaky `NotFoundError` in the full run and was re-run alone (`identity-last.mjs`); its six hashes then matched the branch's (full-size `f34d827d0e1ed7bc…`, as in Part 17). No rendering code changed, so this is the expected result.
- **Unit tests** (`npx vitest run`: **991 tests in 101 files** after merging `main` `0b09b9c`, which has 935 in 98, so 56 new):
  - `develop/sectionEyes.test.ts` (33): every section's hidden form equals what its own Reset gives (written out as each panel does it), touches nothing else (Advanced, Refine Saturation, Point Color with the mixer, Blending, Transform with the crop, …) and gives the same GPU uniforms as the reset photo; all of a group's sections hidden equal the group at its defaults; As Shot; a RAW's default sharpening at low and high ISO, a JPEG's none, an old edit without Detail; lens off and its eye only when applied; no eye on an unedited photo, an eye per changed section, an eye kept while off; a hue-only wheel has none; tooltips; the group eyes unchanged without section eyes; **the group overrides its sections** and other groups' sections still apply; the badge's names; white balance hidden → the RAW stage.
  - `engine/develop/curve-auto.test.ts` (17): percentiles on the stats axis; **an S-curve on a flat histogram** (below the diagonal at 25 %, above at 75 %, the pivot kept); **monotonic** and 0…1; **near identity on a contrasty histogram**; at most slope 1.25; **deterministic**; the ends never moved towards black or white; **no more clipping than the current state** for a straight curve, Strong Contrast, Fade and Regions over seven histograms (including mass at black and white); a faded curve's lifted blacks kept by the blend; only the RGB curve written; nothing on an empty or single-tone histogram; Auto colour **removes a synthetic cast** (red +0.03, blue −0.02: midtone spread 0.05 → under 0.004, highlights to under 60 %), is **bounded** (at most 4 % at the anchors, 4.6 % anywhere), **changes nothing on neutral input**, keeps curves rising and the RGB curve and Regions, is deterministic; the **Amount** blends between the curve before and after (0, 50 %, 100 %, clamped).
  - `develop/curveAutoState.test.ts` (6): **one history step**, its own even after one of the same name; the Amount changes it in place (5 changes, no new step, half-way); 0 % = the curve before, 100 % = the result; undo hides the Amount and moving it then changes nothing, redo shows it; at 0 % the step is never mistaken for the one before; a result that changes nothing adds no step.
- From a clean `npm ci`: `npx tsc -b`, `npx oxlint` (only the two existing warnings, `syscheck/report.tsx` and `LibraryView.tsx`), `npx vitest run` and `npm run build` passed. After the merge the main chunk is 1,168.79 kB (373.88 kB gzipped).

### Limitations and unverified

- **Real GPU:** the RAW stage rebuild for a hidden white balance was only timed on SwiftShader (20–26 s plus a slow drawing); on a real GPU it should take what any white balance change takes (not measured here). Until it is done the view shows the display-ratio approximation.
- **Auto colour reads whole channels**, so a photo dominated by one colour is nudged towards neutral too (seen on the test JPEG's sky), within the 4 % limit. Near-neutral-pixel sampling is a later improvement.
- **Auto contrast's target** (spread 0.32, slope at most 1.25) was chosen by reasoning and checked on the test JPEG only, not tuned on the owner's photos; its clipping check works on the 256-bin luma histogram of the curve's input, so a saturated colour measured differently by the curve's brightness measure (anything but Largest channel) could still reach the display limit; the e2e measured none on the test photo.
- ~~The Crop section's Reset button on `main` also removes Transform~~ — fixed 2026-10-17: it keeps Transform, as the Crop eye does (above). Checked by unit tests and an e2e (1:1 crop + Angle 2.25° + Vertical 10 → Reset: crop and angle back, Vertical 10 kept, one step “Reset crop”; undo brings both back; Transform's Reset then clears Vertical and keeps the crop).
- Lightroom has no section eyes that LightBench knows of (this is LightCraft's group-eye idea extended; not checked in Lightroom). Photoshop's Auto Contrast and Auto Color are mentioned only as names; nothing of their behaviour was checked or copied.

## Part 22: CPU budget, multi-core Remove and overlapped export

**What it does.** Remove's fill now runs on several CPU cores, Export encodes photos on several cores while the graphics card develops the next one, and all of LightBench's CPU-heavy workers share one budget, half the computer's logical cores, with the work a person is waiting for served first. Nothing renders differently: Remove fills are exactly main's (same matches, same weights, same pixels), and exported files are byte-identical to the one-at-a-time export, names and numbering included (Verified, below). No setting is added and no process version is needed.

**Why.** The owner's GPU benchmark on the RTX 4000 Ada laptop (Edge, System Check › GPU benchmark, Full): one Remove spot took 577 ms of wall time at 24 MP (1020 ms at 45 MP) of which the GPU took 0.66 ms, and about 5 s for a real spot on a real photo; an export's GPU work took 342 ms at 24 MP but its jpegli encode 908 ms (1822 ms at 45 MP), one photo at a time. The graphics card was idle most of the time; the CPU steps, each on one core, were the bottleneck.

Owner's decisions (2026-10-14):
1. **Remove on several cores**, with fills byte-identical to main for the same inputs, so old edits never change. Only parts without sequential dependencies are split; what stays sequential is documented. No GPU port (that would need a process version; deferred).
2. **Faster batch export by overlap**: photo N encodes on a pool of encoder workers while the GPU develops photo N + 1; files byte-identical to the sequential export; progress, cancel, per-photo failures and Part 14's GPU rules unchanged; memory bounded.
3. **One CPU budget = half the logical cores** (`navigator.hardwareConcurrency / 2`, rounded down, at least 1), shared by Remove's fill workers, Export's encoders, the culling analysis and Open folder's probing, **foreground first**: Remove, Export and Open folder get workers before background work, which yields between items. Workers are made lazily and ended when idle; pages with 1–2 logical cores still work.

### The CPU budget (`engine/cpu-budget.ts`, `engine/worker-pool.ts`)

| Behaviour | Detail |
|---|---|
| Size | `floor(hardwareConcurrency / 2)`, at least 1. Where the browser doesn't give `hardwareConcurrency` (missing or 0), 4 cores are assumed, as the probe pool always did: 2 slots. `?cpus=N` in the address sets the size (testing, measurements). |
| Leases | A pool takes a lease (`acquire(priority, want, min)`) and keeps at most `lease.slots` workers busy. Leases are live: whenever one is taken or given back the slots are shared again and the pools hear it (`onChange`). |
| Foreground first | Foreground leases (Remove, Export, Open folder, Import) share the whole budget evenly, capped by what each wants (what one doesn't want goes to the others, earlier leases first). A foreground lease always gets its `min` at once (1; Open folder's and Import's probes 2), even if that briefly means more workers than the budget: work a person waits for never waits for background work. |
| Background yields | Background leases (the culling analysis; the one-time refresh of Part 6) share only what the foreground leaves, possibly nothing. Their pools start an item only while a slot is free, so when foreground work arrives the item being measured finishes and the next waits (pause), or fewer run at once (shrink); nothing is stopped half-way. They go on when the foreground gives the slots back. |
| Pools | `WorkerPool`: no worker until a task is queued; a worker is made only when a slot is free and no idle one is left; workers idle for 30 s are terminated (their memory goes with them); the lease is given back 0.5 s after the queue empties, or, with `hold`, kept until the pool is disposed (Export's run). The fill pool (below) manages its own workers the same way (ended after a minute idle) because its workers take roles. |
| Who uses what | Remove: foreground, wants the whole budget, for the fills of one spot list. Export: foreground, wants the whole budget, held for the whole run (also while its encoders wait for the GPU, so the analysis pass doesn't take the CPU from the decode and develop steps). Open folder and Import: foreground, `min(6, max(2, budget))` probe workers, at least 2 (exactly main's count when nothing else runs, so Open folder is never slower). Culling analysis: background, 2 at most. One-time refresh: background, 2 at most. |
| Low core counts | 1–3 logical cores: budget 1. Remove computes each fill whole on one worker (`complete`, as before); Export has one encoder, still overlapping with the GPU (2 photos in flight); Open folder keeps 2 probe workers (main's floor); background passes run on the one slot when nothing else needs it, and wait otherwise. Unit-tested, and the Remove identity was checked in the browser at `?cpus=1`. |
| Not counted | The RAW decoder worker (LibRaw), the retouch worker (applying spots, picking sources), the catalog worker, the main thread and the GPU process. The budget covers the pools that can be widened. |

### Remove on several cores (`engine/retouch/fill-pool.ts`, `fill-split.ts`, `fill.worker.ts`)

Where a fill is computed: before, the retouch worker computed each missing fill itself, in the middle of applying the spots. Now the page's retouch stage (`engine/gpu/retouch-stage.ts`) first asks the retouch worker for the fills' inputs (`prepare`: the same windows of the canonical scene, holes and sources that `runSpots` would use, each key once; `run.ts`, `fillJobs`), computes them on the fill pool, caches them, and then asks the retouch worker to apply the spots with every fill ready (`run`, with no canonical scene). `runSpots` still computes a missing fill itself when given the canonical scene (unchanged; the tests compare both paths).

What runs at once:

| Level | Why it is independent | How it is split |
|---|---|---|
| Fills of different chunks | Several spots, or a long stroke's chunks: each fill is computed from the canonical scene and the spots' shapes only (Part 11), never from another fill. | One task per completion, run as many at a time as there are slots. |
| The two completions of a fill | `complete` computes, for a spot with a source (every Remove spot has one), a completion that starts from the source and one that starts smooth, then keeps the one with the lower score (the source's on a tie). They share nothing but their inputs. | Two tasks; the pool picks between their results exactly as `complete` does (`pick`). |
| Patch vectors | A pixel's vector (√RGB and the 5 × 5 texture means) depends only on the image within 3 rows of it. | By rows (`patchVectorRows`, blocks of 4 rows). |
| Each search | Each target patch's search reads the estimate (unchanged during a search), the valid sources and its own current match, and writes only its own match and distance (`searchRange`). Candidates are visited in the same fixed order and only a strictly better one replaces the best, so a patch's result doesn't depend on who searches it or when. | Blocks of 32 target patches taken from a shared counter. |
| Each vote | Each hole pixel's sums run over the matches in their order, whoever makes them (`voteSums`, by rows of the hole's bounding box); the means are written only after every sum is made (`voteWrite`), so the sums read the estimate before any of it changes, as in one pass. | By rows of the box (blocks of 4), in two steps. |

**What stays sequential, and why** (inside each completion, on its leading worker): the pyramid's levels (each level starts from the coarser level's estimate and matches); the iterations of a level (each searches with the estimate the previous vote made); the weights (a percentile of all the distances); the starts (`healStart` and the smooth start solve Laplace's equation by red/black Gauss-Seidel with over-relaxation, whose updates and stopping test depend on their order); the level set-up, the final distances and the score (novelty and flatness). The two completions of a fill also each build their own levels (the same ones), as `complete` builds a fresh pyramid for each.

**How the workers share work.** With more slots than completions, the extra workers are spread over the completions as helpers (earlier completions get the remainder). The arrays a step needs live in SharedArrayBuffers (LightBench's pages are cross-origin isolated: COOP/COEP headers in development and through the service worker in production). For each step the leading worker writes its parameters into a small control array, opens a new generation and wakes the helpers (`Atomics.notify`); everyone takes blocks from a shared counter until none are left; the leader closes the generation and waits until no helper is still working (`Atomics.wait`), and only then goes on. A helper that arrives after a generation closed does nothing, and the leader does whatever no helper took, so a slow, late or missing helper can never change a result or stall the leader. Helpers leave when the leader finishes, or after 20 s without a step (a leader that was stopped). Where SharedArrayBuffer isn't available, completions still run in parallel, without helpers.

**One slot** (1–3 logical cores): one fill worker runs `complete` itself, one fill after another, exactly as the retouch worker did.

**Also changed, with the same results:** the vote keeps its sums over the hole's bounding box instead of the whole window (the same sums in the same order; it allocated and scanned the whole window, 12.5 MB at 24 MP, on every iteration), and `patchVectors` is written as rows (the same arithmetic). Measured in Node on a 561 × 561 window: the vote went from 134 ms to 59–75 ms in one fill. The fill tests below hold both to main's results.

### Overlapped export (`export/run.ts`, `encoder-pool.ts`, `encoder.worker.ts`, `encode.ts`)

| Behaviour | Detail |
|---|---|
| Pipeline | Photos are decoded and developed one after another on the export's private engine, exactly as before (Part 14's `guarded` work, a fresh engine after a loss, one retry after lost/out-of-memory/internal errors, never after a validation error, `unload()` after each render). Each developed photo then goes to an encoder worker, and the next photo is developed at once. |
| Encoding off the page | JPEG (jpegli: RGBA → RGB, encode, then EXIF/XMP/ICC segments), PNG and TIFF (RGB copy, filter or predictor, deflate with the browser's CompressionStream) run in encoder workers with the same functions and arguments as before (`encode.ts`); the developed pixels are transferred, not copied. EXIF, XMP and the ICC profile are built on the page as before; the TIFF's EXIF DateTime is taken on the page when the photo is developed (it used to be taken when the TIFF was encoded, right after). |
| In photo order | Finishing a photo — its file name from the template (with its sequence number), the destination folder, the conflict rule (`_01`, `_02` …), the write, and the progress — happens strictly in photo order, after the photos before it are finished. So the files, their names, the numbering and the written/skipped/failed lists are those of the one-at-a-time export, whichever photo's encode finishes first. |
| Memory bound | A photo is developed only while fewer than `inFlightLimit` developed photos are waiting to be encoded and written. Each counts its read-back pixels (4 bytes a pixel, 8 at 16 bits) plus the encoder's working memory (jpegli's WebAssembly heap, measured in Node at about 13 bytes a pixel: 314 MB after a 24 MP photo, 587 MB after 45 MP; PNG/TIFF 6 bytes a pixel, 12 at 16 bits), at the largest photo so far, in 2 GiB (a quarter of the device's memory where Chromium reports less than 8 GB). At most one more than the encoders, at least one. So 24 MP JPEGs: 408 MB each, up to 5; 45 MP JPEGs (765 MB) and 45 MP 16-bit TIFFs (900 MB): 2, i.e. one encoding while the next develops; 100 MP 16-bit TIFFs (2 GB): 1, one at a time as before. The limit uses the largest photo developed so far, so a much larger photo late in a batch can exceed it briefly. |
| Encoders | As many as the lease gives (the whole budget when nothing else runs), made on first use; the export disposes of them at the end, which frees jpegli's heaps (before, the one jpegli worker kept its heap for the rest of the session). |
| Progress | `done` counts photos finished, in order, as before; the name shown is the photo being developed. |
| Stop | The dialog's button is now **Stop after the photos in progress**: no new photo is developed; the photos already developed (at most the in-flight limit) are encoded and written. |
| Failures | A photo that fails to load, develop, encode or be written fails alone, in its place in the list, with the same messages as before; the others are written. |
| Benchmark | The GPU benchmark's JPEG row (`encodeJpeg`) uses a shared encoder pool; the pixels are copied to the worker (the benchmark encodes the same image several times) instead of converted to RGB on the page. Its Remove row now says how many fill workers the page may use. |

### Verified

Headless Chromium (Playwright's build) with SwiftShader WebGPU and `?gpu=readback`, Vite development servers of this branch (a copy of its committed source) and of main, side by side, and their production builds for the Open folder timings. The machine has 4 logical cores (budget 2), and other builds' browser tests ran on it at the same time (load average 5–10 during most measurements), so **every time below is noisy and relative only, and none says anything about a real GPU or a many-core CPU**. The "main" these runs compare against is main `153e457` (0.6.1, Snapshots merged), which main had become while this was built; the fill fingerprints were also recorded on `5307029` (0.6.0) before any change, with the same values. The browser runs used this branch before main's soft proofing (0.6.2) was merged into it; the merge had one conflict (PLAN.md) and merged `bench/run.ts` automatically, and the unit tests, type check, lint and build were run after it. Scripts in the scratchpad's `perf/e2e/` folder.

- **Unit tests** (`npx vitest run` after merging main `0b09b9c`: 100 files, 977 tests; main has 98 and 936, so 41 new):
  - `engine/cpu-budget.test.ts` (15): the size (half the cores, rounded down, at least 1; 4 cores assumed when unknown); sharing (even, capped, leftovers to the earlier leases); foreground leases get their share at once and their minimum even over the budget (Open folder's floor of 2); background leases get what is left, hear 0 when foreground work comes and resume when it goes (`whenAllowed`); several background leases share; one slot; release twice. Worker pools: lazy workers up to the slots, terminated after `idleMs`, the lease given back after `lingerMs`; a background pool pauses between items while foreground work holds the slots and never stops an item; shrinks to one worker; `hold` keeps the lease until `dispose`; a worker that failed to start is ended and replaced; `clear` and `dispose`.
  - `engine/retouch/fill-pool.test.ts` (15): **fingerprints recorded on main** for 14 synthetic fills (`fill-cases.ts`: stripes, waves, bricks and noise; a deep hole that uses the coarse grid search; a wire; tiny holes down to one pixel; holes at the corner, the right and bottom edges; several holes; a source outside the window; blocked pixels; patch radius 2; nothing to copy from) and for `runSpots` on 6 spot lists (one Remove spot; at a corner; a tiny one; Remove, Heal and Remove overlapping; a long stroke in two chunks; one at opacity 0 and one at the right edge): `complete` and `runSpots` give main's fingerprints; patch vectors made by rows in two different splits, and searches and votes split and done in reverse order, give the serial completion and score; the pool gives main's fills with 1 slot (serial), 2 slots and 5 slots with shared memory (helpers), all 14 in one call and each alone; progress per fill and the slots given back; a failing worker fails the call and gives its slots back; workers made on first use and ended when idle; fills computed by the pool and applied by `runSpots` give main's pixels; only fills not cached are asked for. (In Node the helpers run after their leader, so they take no work; real concurrency was checked in the browser, below.)
  - `export/run.test.ts` (18, 11 new; the 7 GPU-failure tests of Part 14 still pass, one now loses the engine when photo B's progress is announced rather than when photo A is written): the next photo is developed while one encodes and the files are written in photo order; conflicting names (`_01`) and sequence numbers as one at a time, whichever encode finishes first; progress `done` counts up and `current` names the photo being developed; one photo at a time when a photo wouldn't fit twice in memory; a photo failing to encode, or on the GPU while another encodes, fails alone in its place (validation not retried); Stop finishes what was developed and develops nothing more; Stop while waiting for memory; `photoBytes`, `inFlightLimit` and `inFlightBudget`.
- **Remove fills on real workers, in the browser** (`fill-identity.mjs`): the 14 synthetic fills, all in one call and each alone, with `?cpus=1, 2, 4, 8` (8 = more workers than cores, so helpers really run at the same time as their leaders): **28 of 28 identical to main's fingerprints at every size**; the plans used were serial (1), two completions at once (2), and two completions with 1 and 1, 3 and 3 helpers (4, 8).
- **Export, byte identity** (`export-batch.mjs`, 8 photos: the D700 and D80 NEFs and copies of them, the two test JPEGs and a copy, the ILCE-6300 ARW; three with saved edits, two with Remove/Heal/Clone spots whose fills are computed during the export; a fixed clock): full-size JPEG with camera EXIF and the template `photo` (so the names `photo`, `photo_01` … `photo_07` depend on the order), 16-bit PNG at 1024 px with `$(SEQUENCE[2])_$(FILE.NAME)`, 8-bit TIFF at 50 % with camera EXIF: **24 of 24 files byte-identical** (SHA-256) and the same names, written lists and progress order.
- **Old edits** (`identity.mjs`, 15 cases on the NEFs, the ARW and the JPEG, with crops, angles, spots, Tone Curve, Detail, Colour Mixer and Colour Grading at defaults; full-size and 1024 px export SHA-256, 512 px thumbnail, histogram statistics, the Develop canvas at fit and 100 %): **15 of 15 identical** to main, with no transient `NotFoundError` this time.
- **Export dialog** (`export-dialog.mjs`, 8 photos while the culling analysis measured 60 others, budget 2): **11 of 11**. The heading counted 1 of 8 … 8 of 8 and the bar 0 → 100 % without going back; the 8 files by name; the analysis count went from 12 to 14 (the two photos being measured when the export started finished) and then **stayed at 14 for the 224 s of the export**, and went on afterwards (38 of 68 a minute later); **Stop** once photo 2 was being exported: 2 files written (A01, A02), none failed; with one photo's original deleted: "7 files saved, 1 failed" with "A03.JPG: The photo’s folder isn’t connected …", the other 7 written. No page errors. (A first run lost its development server, stopped by the test harness's time limit, in the middle of the third export: every later photo failed with "The encoder failed to start"; worker pools now end a worker that failed to start and make a new one for the next task.)
- **Remove in Develop** (`e2e-remove.mjs`, Part 14's script, the D700 and D80 NEFs): **14 of 18 on this branch and 14 of 18 on main**, failing the same four "fill under the pin" checks that Part 14 found failing on both builds with the RAW sharpening on (the effect measured over the whole view: centroid 707.1,560.3 over 2322 px on both, as in Part 14). Painting a spot ("Remove spot 1", 1317 ms on the branch, 1401 ms on main), the export's two byte-identical files and the fill where Develop shows it, white balance not changing the fill, the source drag, size keys, Show all, Delete and Ctrl+Z, Copy and the background developer's Paste all passed on both.

**Timings** (SwiftShader, a shared 4-core machine; relative only):

| What | Main | This branch |
|---|---|---|
| Fill alone, 24 MP bench spot (561 × 561 window), source outside; medians of 5, two rounds | 555, 652 ms | 1 worker 586, 646; 2: 474, 517; 4: 572, 676 ms |
| Fill alone, same, source inside the window | 521, 790 ms | 1: 524, 597; 2: 424, 514; 4: 483, 323 ms |
| Fill alone, 45 MP bench spot (771 × 771) | 1050, 1367 ms | 1: 1119, 1200; 2: 800, 982; 4: 742, 1075 ms |
| Remove one spot through the engine (the benchmark's row: 24 MP synthetic Bayer, read-back), medians of 5 | 2099 ms (load ≈ 5); 1737, 1992 ms (load ≈ 7–8) | 2 workers: 1610 ms (load ≈ 5, the first version of this change); 1778, 1671 ms; 1 worker 1673, 1705; 4 workers 1531, 1613 ms (load ≈ 7–8) |
| JPEG encoding alone, 8 × 12 MP, after a warm-up | 642, 582 ms a photo (one jpegli worker) | 1 encoder 599, 584; 2: 360, 338; 4: 301, 334 ms a photo |
| Export of the 8 photos above, full-size JPEG / 16-bit PNG 1024 px / TIFF 50 % | 840 / 807 / 841 s | 853 / 831 / 889 s |
| Open folder, 1,000 JPEGs (production builds, alternating) | 56.8, 50.5, 60.4, 59.8, 25.4 s | 52.8, 56.4, 62.6, 54.3, 64.6 s |
| Open folder, the culling e2e folder (26 files; production builds) | 5.2, 6.0, 4.5 s | 4.9, 2.9, 2.9 s |

What these say, and don't:
- **Remove**: with 2 workers the fill alone took 0.65–0.85 of main's time; with 4 workers anywhere from 0.4 to 1.04 (4 workers on 4 cores that other browsers were also using). Through the engine the gain shrinks further, because on SwiftShader reading the tiles back and writing them is slow, and the spot is applied (`runSpots`, about 100–130 ms in Node) on one core as before. Profiled in Node on the 24 MP window, the patch vectors, searches and votes (the parts that are split) were about two thirds of a fill and the sequential parts (levels, starts, weights, score) about a third, so even with many cores a fill can't take much less than a third of its one-core time. **Not measured: a real GPU and a CPU with free cores**, where the owner's 577 ms (of which 0.66 ms GPU) should fall by roughly what the fill alone falls by here with 2 workers, and further with more; this is an expectation, not a measurement.
- **Export**: on SwiftShader the GPU work is itself done by the CPU and takes about 100 s a photo, against about 1 s of encoding, so overlapping the encoding saves nothing measurable (the batches took 1.5–5.7 % longer, within the noise of the shared machine). Encoding alone scales: 1.7–1.8× faster than main with 2 encoders, 1.7–2.1× with 4. On the owner's GPU, where a 24 MP photo's GPU work took 342 ms and its encoding 908 ms, one at a time is about 1.25 s a photo plus decoding; with 3 or more encoders the encoding of one photo hides behind the development of the next three, so a batch should approach the decode-and-develop time per photo — **an estimate from the owner's numbers, not a measurement**.
- **Open folder**: no measurable difference (the medians are 56.8 and 56.4 s; the spread from the shared machine is ±50 %). On the development servers, before the probe pool started its workers with the pool, four alternating runs took 68.3, 75.1, 64.7, 71.7 s on main and 76.2, 76.6, 70.3, 79.1 s on the branch; the pool now starts its workers when Open folder starts, exactly as main did, and the production runs after that show no difference.
- From a clean `npm ci`: `npx tsc -b`, `npx oxlint` (the two existing warnings, `syscheck/report.tsx` and `LibraryView.tsx`), `npx vitest run` and `npm run build` passed.

### Limitations and unverified

- **No real GPU and no many-core CPU** were available: every speed-up claimed for the owner's laptop is an expectation. The owner's next GPU benchmark run (System Check › GPU benchmark) will show the Remove row with the fill on the budget's workers (its note says how many); the export batch isn't in the benchmark.
- The fill's sequential parts (levels, starts, weights, score; about a third of a fill's time in Node) don't scale; a GPU port of the search (deferred; it would need a process version) or a parallel Gauss-Seidel are the next steps if Remove stays slow.
- The two completions of a fill each build the same pyramid (as `complete` does); building it once and sharing it would save a little CPU, not wall time.
- With helpers, a fill uses SharedArrayBuffer; pages that aren't cross-origin isolated (none of LightBench's) fall back to running the completions in parallel without helpers.
- Overlapping a slow encoder with the GPU uses more memory than one photo at a time (bounded as above; up to about 2 GiB of developed photos and encoder heaps). The memory figures are estimates (the jpegli heap measured in Node, not in a browser); the bound uses the largest photo developed so far, so a much larger photo late in a batch can exceed it briefly.
- The budget doesn't count the RAW decoder, the retouch worker, the catalog worker or the main thread; Remove fills started by the background developer (a Paste with spots) count as foreground work.
- Foreground work may briefly use more workers than the budget (its minimum is never refused); two foreground jobs at once (an export while Remove fills a spot) share the budget evenly.

## Part 23: Masking

**What it does.** Local adjustments. A **Masks** group in the tool stack (after Remove; jump bar "Masks") makes masks and gives each its own sliders: **Temp, Tint, Exposure, Contrast, Highlights, Shadows, Whites, Blacks, Saturation and Vibrance**, plus **Amount**, **Feather** and **Refine**. A mask is made of parts: **Brush**, **Linear gradient**, **Radial gradient**, **Luminance range** and **Colour range**, combined with **Add, Subtract and Intersect**, each part and the whole mask invertible.
